IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE DISTRICT OF WYOMING 
UNITED STATES OF 
AMERICA, 
Plaintiff, 
v. 
JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR., 
Defendant. 


No. 92CR0006B 


eee eee eee ee 


RESTRAINING ORDER 
(Filed Apr. 2, 1992) 


THIS MATTER having come before the Court upon 
the Motion of the United States of America for the entry 
of a protective order pursuant to 21 U.S.C. §§ 846, 848, 
853, and 18 U.S.C. § 982(a)(1), and it appearing to the 
satisfaction of this Court: 


1. That on January 31, 192 [sic], a federal grand jury 
of this District has returned an Indictment charging the 
above-named Defendant with violations, inter alia, of 18 
U.S.C. §§ 1956, AND 21 U.S.C. § 846, 848 and alleging 
that the property named in this Order would, in the event 
of conviction, be subject to forfeiture pursuant to 21 
U.S.C. § 848 (a) and 853 and 18 U.S.C. § 982(a)(1); 


2. That the United States has moved ex parte, for a 
Protective Order pursuant to 21 U.S.C. §§ 848 (a) and 853, 
and 18 U.S.C. § 982(b)(1), to preserve the status quo and 
prevent the above-named Defendant from alienating, 
encumbering, or wasting said forfeitable property; and 


26 


3. That reasonable cause has been shown for the 
entry of a protective order to preserve the property 
named in this Order, based upon the Indictment and the 
Supporting Motion and Memorandum submitted by the 
United States. 


4. It is, therefore, the ORDER of this Court that this 
Protective Order issue and that as to all of the property 
described in Attachment A hereto, incorporated herein by 
this reference: 


The Defendant, JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR., his agents, 
servants, employees, attorneys, family members, those 
persons having any interest or control over the property 
described in Attachment A and those in active concert or 
participation with the above-named Defendant, shall not, 
without approval of this Court upon notice to the United 
States and an opportunity for the United States to be 


heard: 


(a) alienate, dissipate, transfer, sell, assign, 
lease, pledge, encumber, or dispose of, in any 
manner, directly or indirectly; 


(b) cause to be alienated, dissipated, trans- 
ferred, sold, assigned, leased, pledged, encum- 
bered, or disposed of in any manner; or 


(c) take, or cause to be taken, any action which 
could have the effect of concealing or moving 
from the jurisdiction of this Court, or that would 
have the effect of depreciating, damaging or in 
any way diminishing the value of any property 
named in this Order. 


5. It is further ORDERED that if any of the above- 
described property has been or is transferred or disposed 
of by any means, the above-named Defendant shall 


27 


account to the Court for the disposition and location of 
the property. 

6. IT IS FURTHER ORDERED that the financial 
institutions named herein are directed to prevent and are 
otherwise enjoined from transferring (by wire or other- 
wise), conveying or disposing of any monies held in the 
bank accounts described in Attachment A. 


IT IS FURTHER ORDERED that the financial institu- 
tions shall continue to receive and credit monies to the 
accounts identified in Attachment A and any such addi- 
tional funds shall be subject to this Order and frozen 
pursuant to its terms. IT IS ORDERED that the respective 
financial institutions continue to receive and credit 
monies to this account. Any such additional funds shall 
be subject to this Order and frozen pursuant to its terms. 
It is further ORDERED that all financial institutions that 
maintain accounts identified herein shall immediately 
inform the government agents who serve the certified 
copies of this Order what each account balance is on the 
date of service and thereafter to the extent any account 
balance increases. The Court directs that Special Agents 
of the United States Internal Revenue Service, U. S. Cus- 
toms Service, and the Drug Enforcement Administration 
are authorized to serve on the financial institutions 
named herein certified copies of this Order. 


7. IT IS FURTHER ORDERED that the Defendant 
shall be served forthwith a copy of this Order and the 
papers of the United States seeking this Order. 


8. IT IS FURTHER ORDERED that the Defendant 
will be allowed, in the alternative, to execute a satisfac- 
tory performance bond pursuant to 18 U.S.C. § 1963(d), 


28 


and the Court will make the determination if it is a 
satisfactory performance bond, but this Order will be in 
effect and until such time as the Court determines that 


the proffered bond is satisfactory. 


9. This Order shall remain in effect until further 
Order of the Court or until the Court makes modification 
of the Order as appears necessary in the interest of justice 
and consistent with the purposes of 21 U.S.C. § 853 and 
18 U.S.C. § 982. 


DATED this 2d day of April, 1992. 


/s/ illegible 
UNITED STATES 
DISTRICT COURT 
CERTIFIED A TRUE COPY: 
Clerk 


By /s/ illegible 
Deputy Clerk

UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE DISTRICT OF WYOMING 
(Caption Omitted In Printing) 


MOTION TO AMEND ATTACHMENT A 
~~ TO THE RESTRAINING ORDER 


~~ TSSUED BY THE COURT 
~~ ON APRIL 2, 1992 


(Filed Apr. 9, 1992) 


COMES NOW the United States of America, by and 
through Gay Woodhouse, Assistant United States Attor- 
ney for the District of Wyoming, and moves that the 
attachment to the Restraining Order be amended to 
include one additional bank account based upon the fol- 
lowing: 


1. On April 2, 1992, the following account was not 
known to the United States: Account number 429-43-221, 
in the name of Dee Smith d/b/a Legal Research, Ltd., at 
Cole Taylor Bank, 350 West Dundee, Wheeling, IL 60090. 


2. It has come to the attention of investigators for 
the United States that Joe Libretti asked his girlfriend, 
Claudia Porter, to transfer some of Libretti’s funds to 
Joseph Kalady in care of Kalady’s sister, Dee Smith d/b/a 
Legal Research, Ltd., in the amount of $8,000, which 
check was issued on or about February 6, 1992. 


3. Joseph Kalady is a federal prisoner who was in 
the Laramie County Detention Facility with Joseph V. 
Libretti, Jr., during December 1991 and January 1992. 
Joseph Kalady is not an attorney; Kalady is not affiliated 
with an attorney; Kalady is not providing legal represen- 
tation to Joseph V. Libretti, Jr. 


33 


4. Because of the transfer of these funds into the 
account of Dee Smith d/b/a Legal Research, Ltd., this 
account should, therefore, be subject to the Restraining 
Order and added to Attachment A thereto. 


WHEREFORE, the United States prays that the 
attachment to the Restraining Order issued on April 2, 


1992, be amended to include the above-referenced 
account. 


DATED this 9th day of April, 1992. 


Respectfully submitted, 


RICHARD A. STACY 
United States Attorney 


By: /s/ Gay Woodhouse 
GAY WOODHOUSE 


Assistant United States 
Attorney 


(Certificate Of Service Omitted In Printing) 


34 


UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE DISTRICT OF WYOMING 
(Caption Omitted In Printing) 


ORDER GRANTING UNITED STATES’ 
M 
~ ORDER ATTACHMENT A 


(Filed Apr. 9, 1992) 


This matter having come before the Court upon the 
motion of the United States of America to amend the 
attachment to the Restraining Order entered on April 2, 
1992, and the Court being fully advised in the premises, 


IT IS, THEREFORE, HEREBY ORDERED that the 
account in the name of D. Smith d/b/a Legal Research, 
Ltd., which received the $8,000 check from Joseph V. 
Libretti, Jr., the funds held by Claudia Porter, account 
number 429-43-221, in the name of Dee Smith d/b/a 
Legal Research, Ltd., at Cole Taylor Bank, 350 West Dun- 
dee, Wheeling, IL 60090, be added to the attachment to 
the restraining order which is attached hereto and incor- 
porated herein by this reference and hereby restrained 
from transfer, alienation, or diminution. 


DATED this 8th day of April, 1992. 


/s/ Megible

SUPERSEDING INDICTMENT 
(Filed May 22, 1992) 


The Grand Jury charges that: 


COUNT | 


From on or about January 1, 1982, through and 
including on or about March 24, 1992, in the District of 
Wyoming and elsewhere, JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR. did 
knowingly, intentionally, and unlawfully combine, con- 
spire, confederate, and agree together with John J. Biggs 
(Biggs), Brian Salvatore Urso (Urso), Daniel Dixon 
(Dixon), Richard David Schlosser (Schlosser), and George 
Richard Sabell a/k/a “Chinzo”, (Sabell) and with diverse 
other persons both known and unknown to the Grand 
Jury, to commit the following offenses against the United 
States: 


(A) To knowingly, intentionally, and unlawfully 
possess with intent to distribute 5 kilograms or more of 
mixtures or substances containing detectible amounts of 
cocaine, a Schedule II Controlled Substance, in violation 
of 21 U.S.C. §§ 841(a)(1) and 841(b)(1)(A); 


(B) To knowingly, intentionally, and unlawfully dis- 
tribute 5 kilograms or more of mixtures or substances 
containing detectible amounts of cocaine, a Schedule II 
Controlled Substance, in violation of 21 U.S.C. 
§§ 841(a)(1) and 841(b)(1)(A); 


(C) To knowingly, intentionally, and unlawfully 
possess with intent to distribute 100 kilograms or more of 
mixtures or substances containing detectible amounts of 


40 


marijuana, a Schedule I Controlled Substance, in violation 
of 21 U.S.C. §§ 841(a)(1) and 841(b)(1)(B); and 


(D) To knowingly, intentionally, and unlawfully dis- 
tribute 100 kilograms or more of mixtures or substances 
containing detectible amounts of marijuana, a Schedule | 
Controlled Substance, in violation of 21 U.S.C. 
§§ 841(a)(1) and 841(b)(1)(B). 


MANNER AND MEANS OF THE CONSPIRACY 


1. It was a part of the conspiracy that, beginning on 
or before January of 1982, LIBRETTI became involved in 
the business of unlawfully selling and distributing con- 
trolled substances, including cocaine and marijuana, to 
various customers in and around the Denver, Colorado, 
metropolitan area. Over the course of the conspiracy, as 
described in more detail in the paragraphs to follow, 
LIBRETTI established a well organized and highly prof- 
iteble marijuana and cocaine distribution business in 
which he functioned as the principal organizer, manager, 
and supervisor, as well as the primary source of financing 
through which the organization purchased and obtained 
the controlled substances which were ultimately distrib- 
uted by LIBRETTI and other members of the conspiracy. 
Other members of the conspiracy, including but not lim- 
ited to Biggs, Urso, Dixon, Schlosser, and Sabell, acting at 
LIBRETTI’s direction or pursuant to his management, 
supervision, or control, would perform various tasks 
associated with the acquisition, storage, concealment, and 
distribution of said cocaine and marijuana, as well as the 
concealment and laundering of the money representing 
the proceeds of said illegal drug distribution business. 


41 


2. It was a further part of the conspiracy that mem- 
bers of the conspiracy would make arrangements for the 
purchase of quantities of cocaine and marijuana from 
various individuals, both known and unknown to the 
Grand Jury, who were located in Colorado, Florida, and 
elsewhere. On some occasions LIBRETTI would himself 
make the arrangements for such purchases. On other 
occasions one of LIBRETTI’s other co-conspirators would 
make such arrangements while acting on behalf of, or 
pursuant to the direction and supervision of, LIBRETTI. 


3. It was a further part of the conspiracy that, on 
those occasions when supplies of cocaine were to be 
obtained from sources in Florida, various members of the 
conspiracy would travel to Florida and meet with said 
sources at pre-arranged locations such as the parking lots 
of large shopping malls. On these occasions, said mem- 
bers of the conspiracy would accept delivery of said 
cocaine, which was usually in quantities of at least 2 
kilograms, and then transport it via automobile or other- 
wise to LIBRETTI’s residence in Lakewood, Colorado. 


4. It was a further part of the conspiracy that, when 
cocaine or marijuana would be obtained from sources 
within Colorado, LIBRETTI or other members of the con- 
spiracy would take delivery of said cocaine and mari- 
juana either at the residences of said sources or at 
LIBRETTI’s residence in Lakewood, Colorado. These 
sources, among whom was Sabell, would ordinarily be 
paid for said cocaine (which again was usually obtained 
in 2-kilogram quantities) and marijuana (which was 
obtained in multi-pound quantities) at the time of deliv- 
ery, either directly by LIBRETTI or by other co-conspira- 
tors, who would pay with money provided them by 


42 


LIBRETTI. These payments would ordinarily be in 
amounts of between $30,000 to $35,000 per kilogram for 
cocaine. 


5. It was a further part of the conspiracy that, dur- 
ing the time LIBRETTI lived in his home in Lakewood, 
Colorado, cocaine and marijuana obtained from both the 
Florida sources and the Colorado sources would be deliv- 
ered to LIBRETTI's residence in Lakewood, Colorado, by 
Biggs, Urso, or other members of the conspiracy. Upon its 
arrival at LIBRETTI’s Lakewood, Colorado residence, 
LIBRETTI, sometimes with the assistance of other co- 
conspirators, would weigh said cocaine and other con- 
trolled substances, sometimes conduct tests on it to deter- 
mine its purity, and then repackage it for resale. 


6. It was a further part of the conspiracy that, once 
said cocaine and other controlled substances had been 
weighed, tested, and repackaged, LIBRETTI, sometimes 
with the assistance of other co-conspirators, would trans- 
port the controlled substances from his residence in Lake- 
wood, Colorado, to various rented storage lockers and 
safe deposit boxes located throughout the Denver, Colo- 
rado metropolitan area, where they would be stored, 
often along with thousands of dollars in cash represent- 
ing the proceeds from previous sales of such controlled 
substances. These controlled substances would remain in 
said safe deposit boxes or rental storage lockers until they 
were needed by LIBRETTI or other members of the con- 
spiracy for redistribution and resale to their respective 
customers. 


7. It was a further part of the conspiracy that 
LIBRETTI would also utilize two large heavy duty safes 


43 


located in his Lakewood, Colorado residence for the pur- 
pose of temporarily storing quantities of controlled sub- 
stances and large sums of cash. 


8. It was a further part of the conspiracy that, begin- 
ning in approximately June of 1989 when LIBRETTI 
moved from Lakewood, Colwrado, to Green River, Wyo- 
ming, and continuing on or about March 24, 1992, 
LIBRETTI would direct, manage, and supervise the oper- 
ation of his illegal drug distribution business from his 
various residences in Green River, Wyoming, from his 
place of employment, General Chemical Corporation, 
Green River, Wyoming, and from various detention facili- 
ties in Wyoming and Colorado. This would, in part, be 
accomplished in the following manner: Utilizing tele- 
phones located at his personal residences, at his office at 
General Chemical, and at the detention facilities, 
LIBRETTI would call (or receive calls from) Biggs and 
Urso, one or both of whom would be living in LIBRETTI’s 
house in Lakewood, Colorado. Utilizing previously 
arranged code words, LIBRETTI would then direct Biggs 
or Urso to go to specific pay telephones located in Lake- 
wood, Colorado. Then, often using a pay telephone him- 
self in order to further minimize the possibility of law 
enforcement interception, LIBRETTI would talk to Biggs 
or Urso at said previously arranged pay phones and 
provide them with direction and supervision concerning 
the acquisition and disposition of controlled substances. 
The codes used by LIBRETTI, Biggs, and Urso to desig- 
nate specific pay telephones in Lakewood, Colorado, 
included “Mr. Wong’s” which referred to a specific pay 
telephone located at the China Garden Restaurant, 2098 
Youngfield, Lakewood, Colorado, and “Mr. King’s” 


44 


which referred to a specific pay telephone located at the 
King Soopers Grocery Store, 3400 Youngfield, Lakewood, 
Colorado. 


9. It was a further part of the conspiracy that during 
those times when LIBRETTI was in Green River, Wyo- 
ming, Urso would, pursuant to the direction and supervi- 
sion of LIBRETTI, purchase 2-kilogram quantities of 
cocaine from sources located in the Denver, Colorado 
metropolitan area, including Sabell. These transactions 
would usually occur at LIBRETTI’s house in Lakewood, 
Colorado. On these occasions, upon being advised that 
LIBRETTI was ready to purchase an additional supply of 
cocaine, Sabell would meet with his supplier and obtain, 
on a “front” basis, 2 kilograms of cocaine in Lakewood, 
Colorado, where he would deliver it to Urso. Upon 
receiving the cocaine from Sabell, Urso would weigh it, 
place it inside one of the two heavy duty safes in the 
lower level of LIBRETTI’s residence, and then remove 
from the safes the cash necessary to cover the purchase of 
the cocaine, which money had been left in the safes for 
this purpose by LIBRETTI. Urso would then deliver the 
cash to Sabell, who, in turn, would immediately deliver it 
to his source, who would be waiting for Sabell at a 
nearby location. 


10. It was a further part of the conspiracy that 
LIBRETTI would, on a more or less weekly basis, travel 
from Green River, Wyoming, to Denver, Colorado, in 
order to meet with various members of his drug distribu- 
tion organization. During the course of these trips, 
LIBRETTI would examine, weigh, and repackage con- 
trolled substances previously purchased at his direction 
by Urso; deliver quantities of cocaine and marijuana to 


45 


other members of the conspiracy, such as Schlosser, Urso, 
and Dixon, for resale to their respective customers; sell 
quantities of cocaine or marijuana to his own customers; 
and receive money representing previous sales of cocaine 
and marijuana by his co-conspirators and by their respec- 
tive customers. In making such trips to Colorado, 
LIBRETTI would travel both by commercial airline and 
his own personal vehicles. 


11. It was a further part of the conspiracy that, upon 
receiving cocaine and marijuana from LIBRETTI, or from 
Urso or other members of the conspiracy at LIBRETTI’s 
direction, other members of the conspiracy, including 
Dixon, Urso, Biggs and Schlosser, would arrange for the 
resale of said controlled substances to their respective 
customers. These sales would ordinarily be for between 
$1,200 and $1,500 per ounce for cocaine and would often 
be on a “front” basis, whereby the cocaine would be 
delivered to said customers on credit based on the under- 
standing that when the customers had themselves resold 
said cocaine, they would pay the proceeds of said sales to 
Dixon, Urso, Biggs, Schlosser, and LIBRETTI. 


12. It was a further part of the conspiracy that 
LIBRETTI would hoard the proceeds generated by his 
and his co-conspirators’ sales of cocaine and marijuana in 
secret hiding places such as safe deposit boxes and rented 
public storage lockers located in and around the Denver, 
Colorado, metropolitan area, in Green River, Wyoming, 
and in various other places such as, but not limited to, 
Chicago, Illinois. 


13. It was a further part of the conspiracy that 
LIBRETTI and various other members of the conspiracy, 


46 


at the direction and with the assistance of LIBRETTI, 
would seize personal assets of their customers who had 
fallen behind in paying for supplies of cocaine or mari- 
juana previously received by said customers. These per- 
sonal assets, which included automobiles and musical 
instruments, would either be kept by or on behalf of 
LIBRETTI until said customers had paid off their drug 
debts, or would be sold by LIBRETTI, who would, in 
turn, keep the proceeds of said sales to cover said drug 
debts. 


14. It was a further part of the conspiracy that 
LIBRETTI would obtain special federal firearms licenses 
enabling him to purchase a wide variety of otherwise 
restricted fully automatic weapons and silencers, as well 
as unrestricted conventional firearms such as semi-auto- 
matic rifles and handguns, ammunition, and firearms 
accessories. Once the firearms licenses were obtained, 
LIBRETTI would establish a firearms business through 
which, by purchasing hundreds of thousands of dollars 
worth of said firearms, he would be able to conceal and 
launder the proceeds of the drug distribution activities. 
LIBRETTI would store the firearms he obtained in his 
Lakewood, Colorado residence, in various rental storage 
lockers located in Colorado and Wyoming, and in his 
personal residences in Green River, Wyoming. 


15. It was a further part of the conspiracy that 
LIBRETTI would also invest proceeds from his illegal 
drug distribution business in new vehicles, in parcels of 
land located in Wyoming and elsewhere, in his Lake- 
wood, Colorado house, and in various securities and 
interest-generating accounts. LIBRETTI was, on occasion, 
assisted in making said investments by other members of 


47 


the conspiracy located outside Wyoming and Colorado 
who would, at LIBRETTI’s direction, make said invest- 
ments in a manner calculated to conceal LIBRETTI'’s inter- 
est and involvement. As compensation for rendering said 
assistance, said co-conspirators would receive a percent- 
age of the interest generated by said investments. 


16. It was a further part of the conspiracy that, in 
order to prevent the Government from seizing certain 
assets representing the proceeds from his organization’s 
sales of illegal drugs, LIBRETTI would cause other co- 
conspirators and other persons acting as his agents to 
effect the transfer of legal title to said assets from 
LIBRETTI’s name to the names of either other co-conspir- 
ators, friends or various members of his family. 


17. It was a further part of the conspiracy that 
LIBRETTI would utilize his substantial collection of 
machine guns, silencers, and other weapons as a means 
of intimidating, and enforcing discipline among, the 
other participants in his drug distribution organization 
and in order to ensure that none of said particip..nts 
would inform on LIBRETTI or other members of the 
conspiracy to law enforcement. This intimidation would 
occur in a variety of ways, including LIBRETTI openly 
displaying and on occasion openly firing said weapons 
from the back of his Lakewood, Colorado home when 
other members of the conspiracy were present; LIBRETTI 
on occasion actually pointing said weapons at other 
members of the comspiracy while they were present in his 
Lakewood, Colorado home; and other members of the 
conspiracy threatening their customers and suggesting 
that harm could come to said customers through the use 
of LIBRETTI’s firearms or otherwise in the event said 


48 


customers should inform on LIBRETTI’s drug organiza- 
tion to law enforcement. 


18. It was a further part of the conspiracy that, upon 
learning that they were under investigation by law 
enforcement agencies in connection with their drug dis- 
tribution organization, LIBRETTI and various other mem- 
bers of the conspiracy would correspond with each other 
in regard to the stories they should relate to law enforce- 
ment officers in order to conceal the nature, extent, and 
the identity of the participants in said drug distribution 
organization. In addition, LIBRETTI would contact and 
agree to pay for attorneys to represent the interests of the 
other co-conspirators in connection with law enforcement 
investigations or prosecutions of said co-conspirators. 


19. During the course of the aforesaid conspiracy, 
LIBRETTI, Urso, Biggs, Schlosser, Dixon, Sabell, and the 
other members of the conspiracy were responsible for 
possessing and distributing approximately 60 kilograms 
of cocaine and approximately 600 pounds of marijuana. 


OVERT ACTS 


During the course of the aforesaid conspiracy and for 
the purpose of accomplishing the objects thereof, the 
following overt acts were committed: 


1. From on or about spring of 1982, up to the begin- 
ning of 1983, LIBRETTI distributed marijuana on 
numerous occasions in quantities of '/s ounce to '/s pound 
to David Michael Corson in the Denver metropolitan 
area. 


49 


2. From on or about January of 1983, through and 
including on or about August of 1985, LIBRETTI distrib- 
uted about 3 ounces of cocaine per month to Craig David 
Juliano. 


3. Between on or about January of 1984, and on or 
about June of 1987, LIBRETTI distributed 1/8 ounce 
quantities of cocaine in the Denver metropolitan area to 
David Michael Corson on approximately 100 occasions. 


4. On or about October 10, 1985, LIBRETTI sent a 
cashier’s check in the amount of $9,000 to his brother, 
William Libretti, and requested that William Libretti 
obtain a second cashier's check in the amount of $9,000 
from Talman Home Federal Savings and Loan Association 
of Illinois and return it to LIBRETTI. 


5. Between on or about January of 1986, and on or 
about January of 1990, Urso, Dixon, and other co-conspir- 
ators distributed a total of approximately 10 kilograms of 
cocaine in the Denver metropolitan area to Michael 
Patrick Carson. 


6. Between on or about January of 1987, and on or 
about September of 1987, David Michael Corson and 
LIBRETTI distributed cocaine in approximately 1/8 ounce 
quantities in the Denver metropolitan area, to Raymond 
Rivera on approximately 30 occasions. 


7. On or about July 31, 1987, LIBRETTI rented 
locker #A054 at the Public Storage Lockers, 12351 West 
44th Avenue, Wheatridge, Colorado, for the purpose of 
storing large quantities of cocaine, marijuana, and cash 
proceeds from the illegal drug sales undertaken by mem- 
bers of the conspiracy. 


50 


8. On or about September 12, 1987, LIBRETTI pos- 
sessed with the intent to distribute approximately 1 
pound of cocaine and approximately 600 pounds of mari- 
juana, together with approximately $150,000 in drug pro- 
ceeds, in a storage locker in Wheatridge, Colorado. 


§. Between on or about March of 1988, and on or 
about March of 1990, LIBRETTI entered safe deposit box 
#5409 at the Jefferson Bank and Trust, Lakewood, Colo- 
rado on approximately 35 occasions for the purpose of 
storing or retrieving controlled substances and cash pro- 
ceeds representing previous sales of controlled sub- 
stances. 


10. Between on or about June of 1989, and on or 


about August of 1991, LIBRETTI made approximately 57 


airline flights from either Rock Springs, Wyoming to 
Denver, Colorado, or from Denver, Colorado to Rock 
Springs, Wyoming, which trips were for the purpose of 
LIBRETTI’s traveling to and from Denver, Colorado to 
manage, supervise or otherwise control his illegal drug 
distribution organization. 


11. On or about November of 1989, in Lakewood, 
Colorado, LIBRETTI delivered approximately 1/2 kilo- 
gram of cocaine to Schlosser. 


12. On or about February 19, 1990, LIBRETTI dis- 
tributed approximately 4 ounces of cocaine to Dixon. 


13. On or about February 19, 1990, in Colorado, 
LIBRETTI seized Dixon's title to his Porsche automobile 
as collateral for approximately 4 ounces of cocaine which 
Dixon had previously received from LIBRETTI, but for 
which he had not yet paid LIBRETTI. 


51 


14. On or about February 19, 1990, in Colorado, 
Dixon distributed approximately 4 ounces of cocaine to 
Michael Carson. 


15. On or about July 1, 1990, LIBRETTI distributed 
approximately 1/8 ounce of cocaine to Kimberly Barron. 


16. On or about September of 1990, in Lakewood, 
Colorado, Urso delivered approximately $60,000.00 cash 
to Sabell in exchange for approximately 2 kilograms of 
cocaine. 


17. On or about September of 1990, in Colorado, 
Urso delivered approximately 3 kilograms of cocaine to 
Niel Rubano, in order for Rubano to store it for him. 


18. On or about September of 1990, in Colorado, 
Urso delivered approximately 2 kilograms of cocaine to 
LIBRETTI. 


19. On or about January 9, 1991, LIBRETTI flew via 
commercial airline from Rock Springs, Wyoming to 
Denver, Colorado for the purpose of removing controlled 
substances and drug proceeds from certain safe deposit 
boxes in order to conceal them from discovery and sei- 
zure by law enforcement agencies. 


20. On or about January 9, 1991, LIBRETTI had his 


_ safe deposit box at Bank Western in Lakewood, Colorado 


drilled, and removed contraband, illegal drug proceeds, 
and other evidence in order to conceal it from discovery 
and seizure oy law enforcement. 


21. On or about July 23, 1991, LIBRETTI utilized 
funds derived from illegal drug proceeds to purchase a 
$28,900.00 cashier’s check from Vectra Bank in Lakewood, 
Colorado. 


52 


22. On or about August 23, 1991, LIBRETTI utilized 
funds derived from illegal drug proceeds to purchase a 
$65,000.00 cashier’s check from Vectra Bank in Lakewood, 
Colorado. 


23. On or about December 4, 1991, LIBRETTI pos- 
sessed approximately 2 ounces of marijuana in his per- 
sonal locker at the General Chemical Corporation, Green 
River, Wyoming. 


24. On or about December 23, 1991, LIBRETTI exe- 
cuted a quit claim deed which purported to transfer title 
to certain real estate from himself to his father, Joseph V. 
Libretti Sr., in order to conceal his ownership of said 
property and to thus prevent the United States govern- 
ment from discovering and seizing it as proceeds of his 
illegal drug distribution organization. 


25. On or about December 19, 1991, LIBRETTI 
caused a letter to be sent to the Seattle Mortgage Com- 
pany in order to attempt to cause Seattle Mortgage Com- 
pany to transfer ownership of a GNMA certificate from 
JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR. to Joseph V. Libretti Sr., for the 
purpose of concealing LIBRETTI’s ownership interest in 
said GNMA certificate and to thus prevent the United 
States government from discovering and seizing said cer- 
tificate as proceeds of his illegal drug distribution organi- 
zation. 


26. On or about April of 1991, LIBRETTI purchased 
2 green military style ammunition cans from Jax Surplus 
in Fort Collins, Colorado, for the express purpose of 
utilizing those cans to store and conceal cocaine. 


53 


27. On or about September of 1991, LIBRETTI 
buried the aforementioned ammunition cans near a lot he 
owned at the Star Valley Ranch in Thayne, Wyoming, 
which said cans contained approximately 1'/2 kilograms 
of cocaine. 


28. On or about April 26, 1991, LIBRETTI flew from 
Denver, CO to Chicago, IL. 


29. On or about April 27, 1991, LIBRETTI rented 
safe deposit box number 2236 at the Countryside Bank, 
1190 South Elmhurst Road, Mount Prospect, Illinois, for 
the express purpose of concealing evidence of his drug 
trafficking organization. 


30. On or about April 27, 1991, LIBRETTI placed 
$28,060 cash collected from illegal drug trafficking into 
safe deposit box number 2236 at the Countryside Bank in 
Mount Prospect, IL. 


31. On or about December 9, 1991, LIBRETTI called 
Claudia Porter and told her to close out his bank accounts 
at the WyoChem Federal Credit Union for the express 
purpose of transferring and concealing evidence (drug 
proceeds) of his drug-trafficking organization. 


32. On or about December 30, 1991, LIBRETTI sent a 
letter to Claudia Porter detailing how Porter should sur- 
reptitiously enter the General Chemical Plant near Green 
River, Wyoming, and remove cash proceeds of his drug 
trafficking business from a locker where he had previ- 
ously hidden them. 


33. On or about February 3, 1992, LIBRETTI called 
Claudia Porter and directed her to remove $8,000 from 
the account he previously had her conceal funds from, 


54 


and mail it to Legal Research Ltd. in Chicago, Illinois for 
the express purpose of concealing those funds from 
potential seizure by the government. 


34. On or about March 24, 1992, LIBRETTI sent a 
letter to Dee Smith, manager of Legal Research Ltd., in 
Chicago, Illinois, requesting that Smith make a rental 
payment on one of the safe deposit boxes rented by 
LIBRETTI to conceal evidence of his drug trafficking 
organization. 


All in violation of 21 U.S.C. § 846. 


COUNT Il 


Between on or about January of 1984, and on or 
about December 4, 1991, in the District of Wyoming and 
elsewhere, JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR. did knowingly and 
intentionally use and carry firearms during and in rela- 
tion to a drug trafficking offense, to-wit, conspiracy to 
possess and distribute cocaine and marijuana as alleged 
in Count I of this Superseding Indictment, the allegations 
of which are hereby re-alleged and incorporated herein 
by reference, in that, throughout the duration of the 
conspiracy described in Count I, JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR. 
used and carried multiple conventional firearms, semi- 
automatic rifles and handguns, fully automatic weapons, 
and silencers for the purposes of intimidating and enforc- 
ing and ensuring the loyalty of various other subordinate 
members of the conspiracy; of ensuring that persons who 
purchased illegal drugs from himself or from various 
other members of the conspiracy would not inform on the 
organization to law enforcement; of safeguarding and 


55 


protecting supplies of controlled substances; and of safe- 
guarding and protecting the cash proceeds generated by 
the sales of said controlled substances. 


In violation of 18 U.S.C. § 924(c). 


COUNT III 


On or about January 9, 1991, in the District of Wyo- 
ming and elsewhere, JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR. knowingly 
and intentionally traveled in interstate commerce from 
the state of Wyoming to the state of Colorado with the 
intent to promote, manage, establish, carry on, and facili- 
tate the promotion, management, establishment, and car- 
rying on of an unlawful activity, said activity being the 
unlawful possession and distribution of controlled sub- 
stances in violation of 21 U.S.C. §§ 841(a)(1) and 846, and 
thereafter, JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR. did perform and 
attempt to perform acts to promote, manage, establish, 
carry on, and facilitate the promotion, management, 
establishment and carrying on of said unlawful activity. 


In violation of 18 U.S.C. § 1952(a)(3). 


COUNT IV 


On or about December 4, 1991, in the District of 
Wyoming, the defendant JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR. did 
knowingly, intentionally and unlawfully possess a 
machine gun, to-wit, an Actions Arms, UZI frame 9 mm 
model A, serial number SA 28093. 


In violation of 18 U.S.C. §§ 922(0)(1). 


56 


COUNT V 


Between on or about November 13, 1986, and on or 
about January 4, 1990, in the District of Wyoming and 
elsewhere, the Defendant JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR., hav- 
ing received income derived directly and indirectly from 
violations of 21 U.S.C. §§ 841, 846, and 848, as described 
in Counts I and VI of this Superseding Indictment, the 
allegations of each of which are hereby incorporated 
herein by reference, did invest directly and indirectly 
such income and the proceeds of such income in the 
establishment and operation of an enterprise, the activ- 
ities of which affected interstate commerce, that is, dur- 
ing the time period indicated above, JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI 
JR. invested the income and the proceeds of income 
derived from his drug distribution organization as 
described in the above-referenced counts of this Super- 
seding Indictment in the establishment and operation of 
Lakewood Firearms Supply of Lakewood, Colorado, a 
firearms business licensed by the United States Depart- 
ment of the Treasury, Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco & Fire- 
arms, to sell fully automatic, automatic and conventional 
firearms, the activities of which business were under- 
taken and carried out by LIBRETTI in Colorado and in 
the District of Wyoming. 


In violation of 21 U.S.C. § 854. 


COUNT VI 


From on or about January 1, 1984, through and 
including on or about the date of the return of this 
Superseding Indictment, in the District of Wyoming and 
elsewhere, JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI JR., the Defendant 


57 


herein, did knowingly, intentionally, and unlawfully 
engage in a continuing criminal enterprise, in that 
LIBRETTI did knowingly, intentionally, and unlawfully 
commit the following enumerated violations of 21 U.S.C. 
§§ 841(a)(1), 843(b), and 846, which violations were part 
of a continuing series of violations of said sections under- 
taken by LIBRETTI, in concert with at least 5 other per- 
sons with respect to whom LIBRETTI occupied a position 
as organizer, supervisor, and manager, and from which 
continuing series of violations LIBRETTI derived substan-

UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT

BILL OF PARTICULARS AND 
SECOND MOTION TO AMEND ATTACHMENT A 
TO THE RESTRAINING ORDER ISSUED BY 
THE COURT ON APRIL 2, 1992 


(Filed Sep. 15, 1992) 


COMES NOW the United States of America, by and 
through Gay Woodhouse, Assistant United States Attor- 
ney for the District of Wyoming, and moves that the 
attachment to the Restraining Order be amended to 
include two properties owned jointly by Joseph V. Libretti 
Jr. and Claude J. Cismaru and to include the funds trans- 
ferred to others to conceal it from forfeiture described 
below: 


1. It has come to the attention of the United States 
that the equity position of Joseph v. Libretti Jr. has 
increased dramatically in the following properties which 
are owned by him jointly with Claude J. Cismaru and 
which properties were purchased with proceeds of drug 
activities engaged in by the Defendant: 


a. A condominium located at 9430 West Ontario 
Drive, Littleton, Colorado 80123, more particularly 
described as follows: Lot 28, Block 2, Dutch Ridge Subdi- 
vision Filing #2, Jefferson County, Colorado. 


b. A condominium located at 9328 West Ontario 
Drive, Littleton, Colorado 80123, more particularly 


73 


described as follows: Lot 133, Block 1, Dutch Ridge Sub- 


division Filing #1, Exemption Survey #1, Jefferson 
County, Colorado. 


2. Because of a substantial increase in equity in 
these condominiums and the fact that the transfer of such 
properties would net a profit to the Defendant, they 


should be subject to the Restraining Order and added to 
Attachment A thereto. 


WHEREFORE, the United States prays that the 
attachment to the Restraining Order issued on April 2 
1992, be amended to include the above-described seal 
property. 


DATED this 14th day of September, 1992. 


Respectfully submitted, 


RICHARD A. STACY 
United States Attorney 


By: /s/ Gay Woodhouse

UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT

(Caption Omitted In Printing) 


ORDER GRANTING UNITED STATES’ 
SECOND MOTION TO AMEND RESTRAINING 


ORDER ATTACHMENT A 
(Filed Sep. 15, 1992) 


This matter having come before the Court upon the 
motion of the United States of America to amend the 
attachment to the Restraining Order entered on April 2, 
1992, and the Court being fully advised in the premises, 


IT IS, THEREFORE, HEREBY ORDERED that the fol- 
lowing-described properties and funds: 


a. All that lot or parcel of land, together with 
its building improvements, fixtures, attach- 
ments and easements, located at 9430 West

PLEA AGREEMENT 
(Filed Oct. 6, 1992) 


United States of America, by and through Gay Wood- 
house, Assistant United States Attorney for the District of 
Wyoming, and Defendant, JOSEPH V. LIBRETTI, JR., per- 
sonally and by his attorney, Craig, L. Truman, submit the 
following plea agreement and stipulation of facts: 


il 1. The Defendant agrees to plead guilty to engag- 
ing In a continuing criminal enterprise in Count Six of the 
Indictment charging a violation of 21 U.S.C. § 848. 


2. The maximum Statutory penalty for the offense 
is life imprisonment, not more than a $2,000,000.00 fine, 
or both, a $50.00 mandatory special assessment, and for- 
feiture of all known assets as prescribed in 21 U.S.C. § 853 
and assets which are discovered at any later time up to 
$1,500,000. 


Ad 


80 


3. The Defendant acknowledges that by pleading 
guilty to Count Six of the Indictment, he waives various 
constitutional rights, including the right to a jury trial 
and a speedy trial. 


4. The Defendant further acknowledges that he has 
read this Plea Agreement and the Indictment, setting 
forth the aforementioned charges against him, that these 
charges have been fully and carefully explained to him by 
his attorney, and that he fully understands both the 
nature and the elements of the crimes for which he has 
been charged, and the maximum and minimum penalties 
to which he could be subjected in the event he is ulti- 
mately convicted on said charges. 


5. The Defendant agrees to give the Court a com- 
plete, truthful and factual basis for the entry of his guilty 
plea. 


6. The United States agrees that no additional 
charges arising from these incidents or for any violation 
of the law now presently known to the United States will 
be filed in the District of Wyoming. 


7. The United States agrees to recommend at the 
time of sentencing that the Defendant receive not more 
than the mandatory minimum sentence of 20 years as 
required by 21 U.S.C. § 848. 


8. The Defendant agrees to pay the special assess- 
ment amount of $50.00 at the time of sentencing. 


9. Defendant agrees to identify all assets that were 
used to facilitate his criminal activity. Defendant further 
agrees that he will provide complete financial disclosure 


81 


forms requiring the listing of assets and financial inter- 
ests. 


10. The defendant agrees to transfer his right, title, 
and interest in all of his assets to the Division of Criminal 
Investigation of the Wyoming Attorney General includ- 
ing, but not limited to: all real estate; all personal prop- 
erty, including guns, the computer, and every other item 
now in the possession of the United States; all bank 
accounts, investments, retirement accounts, cash, cash- 
ier’s checks, travelers checks and funds of any kind. 


11. The parties understand that the sentencing 
judge is neither a party to nor bound by this plea agree- 
ment and is free to impose whatever sentence he feels is 
justified. 


12. Nothing in this agreement shall limit the United 
States in its comments in and responses to any sentencing 
and post-sentencing matters. 


13. The Defendant agrees that he is pleading guilty 
because he is, in fact, guilty and not for the purpose of 
delay or to protect business associates or family mem- 
bers. In addition, the Defendant agrees not to attempt to 
withdraw his plea of guilty under any circumstances and 
that there are no reasons which support a withdrawal of 


his guilty plea. 


14. It is agreed that no additional promises, agree- 
ments, and conditions have been entered into other than 
those set forth in this document and none will be entered 
into unless in writing and signed by ail parties. 


82 


15. The defendant has been advised of § 1B1.3 of the 
Sentencing Guidelines regarding the use of relevant con- 
duct and establishing sentence and has read the advise- 
ment regarding supervised release attached to this 
statement. 


Dated this 5th day of October, 1992. 


/s/ Joseph V. Libretti, jr. /s/ Craig L. Truman

Notice to Defendant regarding supervised release: 
Guideline sentencing requires a term of supervised 
release in connection with a sentence to confinement of 


83 


more than one (1) year, and the Court may impose super- 
vised release in any other case. 


Supervised release will follow the term of confine- 
ment, may be for any term of years and, if justified by the 
Court, may be for the balance of the Defendant’s life. 
Violation of supervised release may result in confinement 
for the term of years imposed on supervised release 
which would be in addition to time previously served in 
connection with the original offense, with no credit given 
for the time spent on supervision. 


SD3-1 - 5D3.2 - 5D3.3 - 7A1.3 - 7A1.4(b)

[TRANSCRIPT OF PROCEEDINGS, CHANGE OF PLEA] 
[3] PROCEEDINGS 


(Change of plea proceedings 
commenced 10:41 a.m., October 5, 1992.) 


THE COURT: Good morning, ladies and gentle- 
men. 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Good morning, Your 
Honor. 


THE COURT: At this time do you care to report 
to the Court the status of this matter? 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Yes, I do, Your Honor. The 
United States and the defendant have entered into a plea 
agreement in this matter. This essentially is a change of 
plea hearing. The plea agreement basically provides that 
the defendant Joseph E. Libretti, Jr. will plead guilty to 
Count 6 of the Indictment which alleges a violation of 21 
USC Section 848, which is engaging in a continuing crimi- 
nal enterprise in connection with drug distribution net- 
work. It carries a penalty of a 20-year mandatory 
minimum sentence to life confinement, a $2 million fine, a 
$50 special assessment and forfeiture as prescribed in 21 
USC Section 853. 


The defendant has agreed to plead guilty to Count 6. 
He has also agreed to forfeit all the property now in the 
custody of the United States, to report to us additional 
property which we may not know about and to forfeit 
that property as well and to transfer it by virtue of his 
plea on the record here today. 


[4] The United States has agreed that we will recom- 
mend that the defendant receive not more than the man- 
datory minimum sentence of 20 years as required by 21 
USC Section 848. 


We are ready to proceed, Your Honor. 
THE COURT: Very well. 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Your Honor, we will also 
dismissal the remaining counts at the time of sentencing. 


THE COURT: Very well. Now, Mr. Truman, 
you've heard the prosecutor's recitation of the terms of 
the plea agreement. Is that substantially correct from 
your standpoint? 


MR. TRUMAN: That is correct, Your Honor. | 
would only advise the Court of one or two other matters. 


THE COURT: What is that. 


MR. TRUMAN: There is now pending against 
Mr. Libretti in the District of Colorado a silencer count. 
This Court nor this United States Attorney has any power 
over that. However, I've been informed recently by Assis- 
tant Public Defender Rick Williamson of the District of 
Colorado that Assistant United States Attorney George 
Buck has indicated to him that they will be dismissing 
that count. 


I want the Court to know that that has been contem- 
plated. It’s not part of the plea agreement, but | [5] think 
in the terms of completeness, the Court should know that 
is also in the circumstances. Otherwise, Your Honor, that 
which the government has said is substantially our plea 
agreement. 


THE COURT: I think that is probably correct. | 
think we're all aware of the fact that the United States 
Attorney of the District of Colorado is somewhat inde- 
pendent, at least as far as Congress is concerned, and I’m 
not sure that he would necessarily recognize the recom- 
mendations of the United States Attorney for the District 
of Wyoming, but they can recommend dismissal. Is that 
your position? 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Yes, Your Honor. 


THE COURT: Very well. Now, Mr. Libretti, you 
appear before me for the purposes of a change of plea. 
And before we go further with the change of plea, I must 
remind you of your constitutional rights that I’m sure 
you're aware of, but I'll recite them for the benefit of the 
record. 


First, you have a right to a speedy and public jury 
trial, which we're in the middle of. You have a right to be 
presumed to be innocent at that trial, and this presump- 
tion of innocence will stay with you to the very end of the 
trial. 


The United States is required to prove its case [6] 
against you by proof beyond a reasonable doubt, and if 
you or your attorneys feel that the case has not in any 
way been proved against you beyond a reasonable doubt, 
you're entitled to be acquitted by a motion at the end of 
the government's case. Obviously, the Court would have 
to rule on that at that time. 


You have confronted many of the witnesses against 
you, and you're entitled to confront the rest of them 
against you. And by that I mean to see them, to hear 


87 


them, to have Mr. Truman or Mrs. Miller cross-examine 
them. 


In addition to that, you're, of course, entitled to your 
own witnesses, and you can have subpoenaed any wit- 
nesses that you want or any evidentiary exhibits that you 
want. And the aim and object of all of this is that this jury 
that has been presently selected can decide as fairly as we 
can have them do it your guilt or innocence. 


Now, if you plead guilty, it’s going to be the end of 
this trial. We’re not going to go through with this until 
the end of this week, or next weeks, and the jury is not 
going to decide whether you're guilty or not. I'll dismiss 
them after this, and that will be that. You'll have to be 
then interviewed by the probation office. Mr. Bonham is 
here, our chief probation officer in court. And the inter- 
view will be arranged and then a presentence report, 
which will probably consist of 20 or more {7] single- 


spaced typewritten pages, takes about a month to prepare 
one of those. 


The probation office checks with everybody and 
doesn’t leave a stone unturned. Our probation office is, I 
think, the best in this circuit, and probably one of the best 
in the United States. 


You and Mr. Truman will have the opportunity to 
review that to be sure that it’s substantially correct, and 
you'll have an opportunity to file objections to it, which 
I'll hear at the time of sentencing , but you will not have a 
continuing trial. You'll just be brought back for purposes 
of sentencing. 


= 


And I think you heard Mrs. Woodhouse tell you what 
the minimum sentences are, the consequences of your 
plea. These include a minimum sentence of 20 years, and 
it could be until life. In addition to that, you could be 
fined $2 million and be required to pay a special assess- 
ment of $50, but all the property that’s described in this 
Count 6 could be forfeited to the United States. So, obvi- 
ously, the consequences of this are very serious. 


Do you feel you understand the consequences of it? 


THE DEFENDANT: There is only one part | 
didn’t understand. They said that they can take up to one 
and a half million dollars from me any time. Let’s say 20 
years [8] from now I get a job, I get a paycheck. They can 
come and take that? 


MR. TRUMAN: Your Honor, may I speak to 
that? 


THE COURT: Yes. 


MR. TRUMAN: The purpose, as I understand of 
that circumstance, is that Mr. Libretti is going to tell us 


under oath that all the assets that he has have now been | 


made known to the government. The reason for that 
clause was that if in the next couple of years should a 
safety deposit box with assets that Mr. Libretti - 


THE COURT: Swiss bank account or something 
that became known. 


MR. TRUMAN: Prior to today’s date become 
known, that the government may seize that. 


THE COURT: Yeah. 


MR. TRUMAN: Your Honor, I think it is fair to 
state from this date forward that Mr. Libretti earns it, if 
it’s earned in a lawful manner. 


THE COURT: I think 20 years from now should 
you have an inheritance from your family that is not drug 
tainted, and I think that property would be free of forfei- 
ture. But I think that as Mr. Truman says, if something 
came to light that you had a secret bank account on the 
Caymen Islands or in Panama or Switzerland or some- 
place like that and you got the money back into this [9] 
country, I think they could go after that and forfeit it. I 
believe that’s what it is. 


As I understand it, I think it has to be the product of 
a drug transaction to be forfeited. Isn’t that your under- 
standing, Mr. Truman? 


MR. TRUMAN: That's my understanding. 


THE COURT: Yes. I think that’s correct. Well, 
now, Mr. Libretti, there is one other understanding that 
I've got to make you understand and that is if you plead 
guilty, I’m going to have to place you under oath, and I 
will have to question you as to whether or not your guilty 
plea is a voluntary plea and whether or not there’s a 
factual basis for the plea. 


Now, under the Fifth Amendment to the Constitu- 
tion, you have a right to remain silent, having been 
accused of crime, and nobody can force you to plead 
guilty. However, if you choose to stand on your right, I, 
of course, won't be able to take your plea and then we'll 
go forward with the trial and I'll call the jury back in and 
we'll start where we left off. 


“= 


90 


So I want to be sure, do you understand these rights? 
THE DEFENDANT: Yes. 


THE COURT: Do you have any questions about 
them? 


THE DEFENDANT: No. 


[10] THE COURT: Are you ready, then, to plead 
to this Indictment? 


THE DEFENDANT: Okay. 


THE COURT: Mr. Truman, is there any reason 
why you’re client should not? 


MR. TRUMAN: I know of no such reason. 


THE COURT: Very well. Joseph V. Libretti, Jr.,

PROSECUTOR’S STATEMENT 
(Filed Nov. 13, 1992) 


COMES NOW, the United States of America, by and 
through Gay Woodhouse, Assistant United States Attor- 


r the District of Wyoming, and for its prosecutor's 
ence as it relates to 


ney fo 
statement briefly summarizes evid 


Joseph V. Libretti Jr. as follows: 


1. In 1981, Joseph V. Libretti Jr. (Libretti) moved 
from Mt. Prospect, Illinois, to Denver, Colorado, to attend 
Denver University with a major in hotel/restaurant man- 
agement. In 1982 Libretti began his drug distribution 
network in the Denver, Colorado, metropolitan area first 
distributing primarily marijuana and shortly afterwards, 


cocaine. 


2. This drug distribution network continued until 
January, 1992, in the District of Wyoming, in Colorado, in 
Florida, and elsewhere. 


3. In about 1984 and 1985, Libretti obtained his 
cocaine from Scott Raymond of Denver, Colorado, who 
was arrested in about 1985. Raymond testified at the trial 
that he sold at least 5 kilograms of cocaine to Libretti. 


4. During 1985 and continuing through 1986, John 
Biggs acted as Libretti’s supplier for cocaine. Biggs 
obtained at least ten kilograms of cocaine for Libretti 


during this time period.

7. In about 1986 Daniel Dixon began purchasin

to Michael Carson. Dixon and Carson were ap rehended

10. From about 1984 until July of 1987, Libretti sold 
cocaine in one to two and a half ounce quantities to Dave 
Corson a/k/a “Frosty” for redistribution to others. One 
of Corson’s customers was Raymond Rivera who later 
became a direct customer of Libretti. 


11. Raymond Rivera (Rivera) observed we 
entering a Public Storage locker in early September, 19 ’ : 
Rivera later returned to the storage locker and broke into

also admitted that he was a drug dealer and that te 
“thought about killing” Rivera when Libretti learned that 
Rivera had stolen the money and drugs from the storage 


locker. 


12. In August of 1987, Libretti opened a safe deposit 
box with his brother, William, in which he left $48,000 in 
cash. In early 1989, Libretti began to instruct William on 
what to do with tne cash, some of which was returned to 
Libretti and some of which was to be invested by Wil- 
liam. At one time, Libretti also placed approximately 
$10,000 in an account with his brother, Timothy’s name 


125 


and social security number on it in order to avoid having 
the interest show up under Libretti’s name. 


13. Libretti continued to be actively involved in 
cocaine and marijuana distribution, using Richard Dave 
Schlosser (Schlosser) to distribute cocaine for him in the 
Loveland, Colorado, area. Claude Jay Cismaru was also 
actively involved in distributing marijuana and small 
quantities of cocaine for Libretti. John J. Biggs was also 
involved in providing Libretti with cocaine from Florida 
and for distributing some of Libretti’s cocaine. 


14. In June, 1989, Libretti moved to Green River, 
Wyoming, and began working at General Chemical in a 
temporary position in the accounting department. In late 
August, 1989, Libretti was hired full-time by General 
Chemical. Libretti travelled from Green River to Lake- 
wood on an almost weekly basis in order to continue to 
manage and supervise his drug distribution network. 
During the summer of 1989, Libretti acquired a new 
source of cocaine, George Richard Sabell a/k/a Chinzo 
(Sabell), who’s [sic] function was to provide 1 to 2 kilo- 
gram quantities of cocaine to Libretti through Brian S. 
Urso, an associate in the drug business of Libretti since 
about 1987. Libretti directed, via the telephone, the 
actions of Urso in connection with the purchases of 
cocaine from Sabell. Libretti instructed Urso to use the 
pay telephones at the China Garden Restaurant and the 
King Soopers in the area near 2440 Willow Lane. Libretti 
would contact Urso at the 2440 Willow Lane address and 
direct Urso go to one of the two pay telephones, desig- 
nated by a code set up by Libretti of “Mr. King’s” for the 
pay telephone located at the nearby King Soopers, and 
“Mr. Wong’s” for the pay telephone located at the China 


126 


Garden Restaurant. Urso would not engage in a cocaine 
transaction with Sabell until the price and the delivery of 
the cocaine had been approved by Libretti. 


15. In about July of 1989, Urso, who had been an 
associate of Libretti’s, began to purchase cocaine for 
Libretti from George Richard Sabell a/k/a Chinso 
(Sabell). On the first occasion, Libretti, through Urso, 
purchased one kilogram of cocaine from Sabell for a price 
of approximately $28,000. After the first buy, Sabell 
would provide two kilogram quantities to Libretti, some- 
times dealing directly with Urso and Libretti, and other 
times, dealing with Urso. Sabell provided two kilogram 
quantities of cocaine approximately every six weeks 
between July, 1989, and December, 1990 for a total of 
approximately 24 kilograms. At the time, Urso was living 
in Libretti’s house at 2440 Willow Lane, Lakewood, Colo- 
rado, from about February, 1989, through the end of 1990. 


16. Dixon was arrested on drug charges in February, 
1990, with Michael Carson, but Dixon was released and 
not charged. Dixon was released after a preliminary hear- 
ing in which the judge found that there was not sufficient 
evidence of Dixon’s involvement in the transaction to 
bind Dixon over. This lack of evidence was primarily due 
to the fact that Carson would not implicate Dixon in the 
drug transaction. In fact, Carson believed that if he did 
implicate Dixon or Dixon’s supplier, Carson would be 
killed. Dixon had gone into great lengths to tell Carson 
about his (Dixon’s) supplier, “Joe’s” arsenal of fully auto- 
matic weapons and his expertise at using them. 


17. Because Dixon was released, Libretti and Biggs 
had discussions about killing Dixon because they 


127 


had witnessed a Sabell delivery of cocaine in which 
Libretti, Sabell and Urso were all present; Dixon had also 
seen substantial sums of cash lying around the house and 
was aware of who some of Libretti’s other distributors 
were. Libretti agreed to pay $50,000 to Biggs if Biggs 
would kill Dixon. At one point in the spring of 1990 
Dixon saw Biggs at a bar and took a ride with Biggs. 
Dixon jumped out of the car when Biggs went into : 
desolate area outside the Denver city limits because 
Dixon believed that Biggs intended to harm him. 


18. Libretti’s gun business was used t 
drug proceeds and was primarily a “front” os _ 
The gun business was a way for Libretti to justify his 
income since Libretti was not working at times during the 
conspiracy and, when he was working, was not bringing 


in the money that would pa 
y for the Lak 
and other investments. ee 


19. The first of Libretti’s recorded gun transactions 
was an illegal transfer of a firearm to Biggs, using the 
alias of William Demetro. .

21. Many of Libretti’s gun transactions were to peo- 
ple who were involved with him in the drug trade (Biggs, 


128 


Schlosser, Sabell, Dixon, Urso and others). Libretti also 
knew that these people and others were drug users as 
well and yet in each instance, Libretti allowed them to lie 
on what is known as am ATF (Alcohol, Tobacco and 
Firearms) Form 4473 when they were asked to check if 
they were illegal users of or addicted to narcotic drugs. 


22. While Libretti was in Green River, he used two 
deputies in the Sweetwater County Sheriff's office to lie 
on letters stating that the Sweetwater County Sheriff's 
office was interested in purchasing fully automatic 
weapons, when the Sheriff's Department had no budget 
for and no to intent to purchase any weapons. Due to the 
complicity of these two sheriff's deputies, Libretti was 
able to obtain approximately 37 fully automatic firearms. 
The sheriff’s deputies also signed a number of letters 
which enable Libretti to obtain many other semi-auto- 
matic firearms which appeared to have been transferred 
to sheriff's deputies, but were merely kept in Libretti’s 
arsenal as were most of the fully automatic firearms. 
Libretti gave one of the deputies a gun in exchange for 
his assistance with these letters. 


23. During the time that Libretti owned the resi- 
dence located at 2440 Willow Lane in Lakewood, Colo- 
rado, Libretti, Biggs, Urso, and Scott P. Matheu, among 
others, fired silenced and unsilenced fully automatic 
weapons off the back porch area of Libretti’s home in 
Lakewood, Colorado. This would often occur during, or 
shortly after, drug transactions and/or drug usage had 
occurred at Libretti’s Lakewood, Colorado residence. 


24. Libretti acquired two large safes in his Lake- 
wood, Colorado, home between 1987 and 1989. Libretti 


129 


used these safes to store large sums of cash, fully auto- 
matic and semi-automatic firearms, many of which were 
loaded, and kilogram quantities of cocaine. Libretti, Urso, 
Biggs, Sabell, Schlosser, and Dixon all witnessed the con- 
tents of the safes and conducted some of the drug trans- 
actions around these safes when they were open. 


25. Libretti stored large sums of cash and cocaine in 
safe deposit boxes in various banking institutions around 
the Denver, Colorado, metropolitan area and in Mount 
Prospect, Illinois. Frequently, Libretti would travel with 
other members of the conspiracy, Dixon, Schlosser, and 
Biggs, to these banks to obtain drugs from these safe 
deposit boxes. Sometimes during trips to the safe deposit 
boxes, wherein Libretti would pick up cocaine and cash, 
Libretti would have loaded guns on or about his person 
and vehicles. In addition, Libretti stored controlled sub- 
stances, an illegal machine gun and a cashier's check for 
$65,000 which represented drug proceeds in his personal 
use locker at General Chemical in Green River. 


26. In Libretti’s home at 2240 Willow Lane, Lake- 
wood, Colorado, a number of loaded firearms were 
located throughout the house. All co-conspirators were 
aware of the existence of these firearms. 


27. Libretti used his firearms business to launder 
money received by him as a result of the distribution and 
sales of cocaine and marijuana. 


28. Libretti also laundered drug proceeds by pur- 
chasing several automobiles, real property in the state of 
Wyoming, and other investments. 


130 


29. Libretti directed the manner in which deals 
would be done with Dixon. Libretti, of course, always set 
the price and the time the deal would occur. Libretti 
directed Dixon with regard to Dixon’s customers contin- 
ued purchases and payments for cocaine. At one point, 
Carson, one of Dixon’s customers fell behind in his pay- 
ments for cocaine which had been fronted to Carson by 
Dixon. Dixon and Libretti discussed what to do about 
Carson's debt; Libretti told Dixon to continue to supply 
Carson with “the product” because Carson could not get 
out of debt without cocaine to sell to others. Later, when 
Carson’s debt became larger, Libretti told Dixon to take 
something of Carson’s which had some value. Dixon used 
Libretti’s truck to seize and sell Carson’s drums and 
keyboard as collateral for the cocaine debt. Libretti also 
required that the payment for cocaine be “faced” (stacked 
with all faces up) and in larger denominations (at least 


“tens”). 


30. Libretti also directed the drug distribution 
efforts of Schlosser. Libretti told Schlosser not to sell 
drugs from his (Schlosser’s) home and not to store drugs 
at Schlosser’s house. Libretti told Schlosser to get a stor- 
age locker and a safe deposit box in which to store 
cocaine and cash, to relocate them periodically, and not to 
enter them too frequently. Libretti required that Schlosser 
use pay phones in order to have certain conversations 
with Schlosser concerning drug deals. Libretti frequently 
contacted Schlosser to see how his sales were going and 
whether or not Schlosser was ready to “re-up” (purchase 
additional quantities of cocaine.) Libretti invented a ficti- 
tious machine shop business for Libretti and Schlosser to 


131 


be partners in, in order to help Schlosser explain Schlo- 


sser’s frequent trips to Lakewood and extra j 
~p extra income to 


31. Libretti directed Biggs’ trips from Florida to Col- 
orado to provide Libretti with cocaine. Libretti would 
instruct when the trips should be made and always dic- 
tated the amount of the deliveries (no more than two- 
kilogram quantities per trip.) 


32. Libretti directed the actions of i iri 
cocaine from Sabell for Libretti. ee ene

Wyoming to weigh, divide, and distribute the cocaine to 
Schlosser, Dixon, and others. Libretti provided Urso with

address book contained the telephone numbers for King

Interestingly enough, Libretti’s address book also con-

34. On about August 21, 1991, Libretti sent a letter 
to Urso who was at that time incarcerated at the Limon 
Correction Facility in Limon, Colorado. The letter speci- 
fically told Urso that when contacted by federal investi- 
gators, Urso should say that the only relationship 
between Urso and Libretti was that of landlord and ten- 
ant and although they did talk on a number of occasions 
over the telephone, the substance of the conversations 
was always landlord-tenant matters. In other words, tell- 
ing Urso what lie to use to explain the frequent telephone 
contacts between Urso and Libretti when questioned by 
federal agents. Libretti sent a subsequent letter to Urso 
(verified by handwriting exemplar identification) which 
was sent via federal express with the return address of 
Cohen, Cohen and Cohen, 1221 East 12th Street, Chey- 
enne, Wyoming 81234 phone number 307-634-0112. Inside 
the federal express mailer was a typed letter to Urso from 
Joe telling Urso what to do when questioned before the 
federal grand jury in Wyoming (invoke the fifth amend- 
ment). 


133 


35. Libretti also hired attorneys or attempted to find 
attorneys and offered to help pay for attorneys for most 
of his co-conspirators when they were questioned by the 
grand jury or federal agents and some when they were 
arrested on drug charges. 


36. On about November 21, 1991, Libretti was sub- 
poenaed by the federal grand jury to produce handwrit- 
ing samples for comparison with other documents seized 
during the execution of a number of search warrants on 
Libretti’s residences and storage lockers. Libretti’s hand- 
writing on that occasion was found by the handwriting 
expert at the Wyoming State Crime Lab to be deceptive. 
Libretti took as long as 45 seconds to sign his name which 
is one of the indications of deception. His handwriting on 
this occasion was significantly different from the hand- 
writing seen on numerous checks, contracts which were 
notarized, legal documents, personal diaries, notes from 
classes and courses and other documents which were 
witnessed to be Libretti’s handwriting. Libretti’s original 
signature on his motel receipt at La Quinta on the night 
prior to giving the handwriting samples was again the 
same (and significantly different from that given by him 
when he provided the handwriting exemplars) as the 
handwriting in the possession and handwriting obtained 
on December 4, 1991, during the execution of a search 
warrant on Libretti’s office at General Chemical. 


37. While Libretti was under investigation, he 
buried one and a half kilograms of cocaine adjacent to his 
property in the Star Valley area near Thayne, Wyoming. 
After this cocaine was seized, Libretti wrote a letter 
(handwriting verified that it was Libretti) to Joe Kalady 
(an inmate/jailhouse lawyer/friend of Libretti’s) stating 


134 


that if the government did not find fingerprints on the 
containers, Libretti would “simply claim” that the dope 
was buried by Schlosser (who advised the government 
that Libretti had purchases ammunition cans to effect 
such a burial.) 


38. There are additional sums of money Libretti 
received from his drug business which have not been 
mentioned. Libretti had approximately $12,000 in cash 
hidden in a paint can in the basement of his girlfriend, 
Claudia Porter’s house when he was arrested. Libretti 
also had a $28,000 cashier’s check in August of 1991 and 
more than $28,000 in cash in his safe deposit box in Mt. 
Prospect, Illinois. There is evidence that Libretti travelled 
to Ann Arbor, Michigan and to Portland, Oregon with 
substantial amounts (perhaps as much as $100,000 on 
each occasion) of cash, none which has been located by 
law enforcement authorities to date. After Libretti was 
arrested, he directed Claudia Porter to transfer approx- 
imately $9,500 to Joseph Kalady in order to keep it from 
federal authorities. Libretti also transferred his property 
in Star Valley to his father and attempted to transfer some 
of his investments to his father in order to keep federal 
authorities from forfeiting or seizing these assets.

[TRANSCRIPT OF PROCEEDINGS, SENTENCING] 
[3] PROCEEDINGS 
(Hearing proceedings commenced 
11:15 a.m., December 23, 1992.) 


THE COURT: Case of the United States versus 
Joseph V. Libretti. Jr. 


Miss Woodhouse? 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Good morning, Your 
Honor, Mr. Truman. We are here this morning in the 
sentencing of United States of America versus Joseph V. 
Libretti, Jr., criminal Docket number CR-92-0006 B. Plain- 
tiff Libretti is here with his counsel Craig Truman and 
Linda Miller for the sentencing. 


As the Court remembers, we started trial on this 
matter September 28th, and Mr. Libretti chose to plead 
guilty pursuant to a plea agreement on October 5th, and 
he pled guilty to a violation of being engaged in a con- 
tinuing criminal enterprise which is prohibited by 21 USC 
848. 


THE COURT: Thank you, Ms. Woodhouse. The 
first matter I will take up are the objections on behalf of 
the defendant to the presentence report. 


Mr. Truman, would you care to proceed on that 
issue? 

MR. TRUMAN: Your Honor, we acknowledge 

the presentence investigation done by Mr. Schoen from 

[4] Mr. Bonham’s office. Your Honor, we agree that the 


calculation of the sentencing guidelines is correct; that 
there is a two-level enhancement for obstruction of justice 


137 


and a two-level mitigation for acceptance of respon- 
sibility. Your Honor, that puts us at a level 38.

[8] MR. LIBRETTI: Well, with that in mind, I’m

That’s not my place. I’m just going to read something I've

I have always accepted responsibility for the mis- 
takes I have made and have tried to make the best - I 
have tried to the best of my ability to make amends and 
restitution to anyone who has suffered an injury because 
of my actions. Any damage I have caused I have repaired. 
My conduct for which I am here today demands no less. I 
am sorry for the actions which bring me before the Court. 
I can accept the punishment myself, but it’s most difficult 
knowing the toll on those who love me. I never imagined 
the effect or devastation my actions would have on my 
loved ones, especially my family. 


141 


The probation department recognizes that my crime 
is a victimless crime, and I am glad that I have never hurt 
anyone. However, I feel that there is a social cost associ- 
ated with drug abuse just as there is with gambling 
addictions and alcohol and tobacco abuse. As an act of 
restitution, | would like to provide some service to soci- 
ety in an effort to alleviate some of the costs of drug 
abuse to the country. 


I have no real understanding of drug addiction, [9] 
I've never been in that situation, and I find it difficult to 
have a lot of compassion for anyone who admits getting 
high. His or her highest priority, more important than his 
or her family and financial obligations. That type of 
behavior appalls me. And it’s probably for this reason | 
would not make a very good drug counselor. But on - on 
the many months I’ve been in jail, I've come to realize 
that my conduct with controlled substances may have 
caused or certainly contributed to others’ irresponsibility 
with drugs. And that does trouble - that does trouble me. 
That was never my intent. 


However, as of this moment, over 14,000 children are 
orphaned due to their parents dying of AIDS. In just a 
little over seven years, in the year 2000, 82,000 children 
will be orphaned due to their parents dying of AIDS. 
Who’s going to take care of these children? I have great 
compassion for these orphaned children. Many of the 
victims of AIDS contracted AIDS through IV drug use. | 
would like to take care of these children with no parents. 


This is probably the best form of restitution I could 
make. I am asking you to find that the degree of my 
conduct, viewed in consideration with the conduct 


142 


required to fall within the charge of engaging in a [10] 
continuing criminal enterprise. The need for parity 
among the defendants with similar records and similar 
conduct and the need for the sentence imposed may 
warrant and be better served with a downward depar- 
ture. 


I did break the law and I'd like to make it right. 
Being in prison won't necessarily make that happen. The 
considerations and needs that require sentence be 
imposed may be better served with the sentence in closer 
parity to the codefendants’ sentences. 


I realize some of the needs for the sentence imposed 
have to do with reflecting a seriousness of the offense. | 
interpret that to mean my conduct, to promote respect for 
the law and to provide just punishment. To provide for 
adequate deterrents to criminal conduct to others and 
myself, and to protect the public, and I'd like to add that 
I’m not a threat to the public, and even Claudia Porter 
says so. And I think if anyone would say something bad 
about me, she probably would. 


So, with that, all I ask is that, you know, the purpose 
of the sentence be kept in mind and perhaps one question 
that you could answer and keep in mind is what objective 
is to be served with my incarceration and can that objec- 
tive be better served with the sentence in [11] closer 
parity to my codefendants? 


I stand ready for acceptance of sentence and all I can 
ask is for fairness. But fairness to me means a sentence 
proportionate with my actions and consistent with the 
actions and sentences of others in this case. And that’s 
what I have to say.

I always hate to come for sentencings. Sentencing is 
always the hardest day of my calendar, particularly this
