Judge, I want to talk a little bit about the Joe Libretti | 
know because he is an enigma to me. In almost 20 years 
of the practice of iaw, exclusively criminal - and I know 
the Court can feel this too - you think you've seen 
everything once. You see the good and the bad in people, 
sometimes the best and the worst [12] of people. Rarely 
do you see a person that’s impossible to figure out. 


Judge, I've tried for a year and I find Joe Libretti hard 
to — to define. In many respects the Joe Libretti I know, I 
like. In many respects the Joe Libretti I know I admire. 
There is a down side of his personality, which I don’t 
understand. You look at a fellow whose family, a man 
couldn’t ask for a better family. All families have prob- 
lems. And the Libretti family is no different. Perhaps one 
family is too warm and another family is too cold, but a 
man couldn't ask for a better family. Come to know all of 
them over this period of time. The sadness of this case 
that strikes their heart is what I find technically poignant 


144 


and has caused Joe such despair that it’s the only thing 
about this case that he can barely stand. 


The prospect of years in custody, the shame of what 
Joe’s actions have done, again, the best and the worst, I 
see a family that forgives him unconditionally, even more 
than Joe forgives himself, for his bad actions and his 
granddad and his mom are here today pulling for him. 
Joe’s found it difficult to communicate with them because 
he is so ashamed and saddened by the predicament that 
he’s put them in. 


An enigma to me also is the love that Joe has [13] for 
his family, and yet his actions of including his brother 
and some of the monkey business around this crime. It'll 
be many days before | forget his brother’s testimony in 
the trial and watching him outside in the hall as he broke 
down sobbing uncontrollably. It will be many days before 
I forget Joe Libretti steeling his jaw so that he built the 
shell of armor around himself while his brother testified. 
And yet I know him well enough to know that his heart 
was breaking inside. I don’t know how Joe came to get in 
this business. I wish I did. I wish we could fix it, but we 
can’t. 


There are several things I do know and that is that 
Joe, an over achiever, a fellow while he’s one of the more 
intelligent people I've ever represented, goes beyond that 
by working hard. And while that was such an attribute as 
a youngster and such an attribute at school, those virtues 
became detriments when he got in the drug business. 


Joe Libretti has always been able to do well no matter 
what he’s done. If he had fastened his star on legitimate 
legal business, what a powerhouse he'd be. 


145

cesspool of drug use. 


I like to think I'm not naive, but I was shocked by

on campus to everybody.

THE COURT: I think I have no alternative but 
[21] to grant the forfeiture order and it is hereby granted. 


Mr. Libretti, I have considered the presentence report 
and, of course, also taken into account all the things that 
you said. I find there’s hardly anything in — as to what 
Mr. Truman has said with which I don’t agree. I question, 
maybe, in some ways the timing of this sentencing just 
before Christmas, and yet I feel that it’s for your own 


151 


advantage that you get out of the county jail and get to 
the institution where you are going to be and to get on 
with that life. 


I have been to most of the institutions west of the 
Mississippi to see for myself what they are like and I have 
generally observed that there are people who work in the 
prison industries like yourself, can be accountants for 
them and do meaningful work in those places you obvi- 
ously can’t do in the county jail. And I think you ought to 
get on about it. So that’s the justification for this kind of 
sentencing. 


Plus the fact there is also a day that I had some time - 
yes, Mr. Truman, you're right, there is nothing harder 
than a sentencing. And throughout the years that I’ve 
been on this bench, I have devoutly prayed that I may be 
a wise and just judge. And yet when I come up against a 
mandatory minimum guideline sentence, I do indeed 
agree that they are harsh and it [22] is not equitable. And 
I am glad that Judge Keene of Denver and I were the only 
two in this circuit that spoke against these laws at the 
time the sentencing commission was considering adopt- 
ing them, just for that reason. 


And I think, Mr. Libretti, that if I were to have the 
discretion that I used to have before Congress took it 
away from me, I would make the sentence in this case 
substantially less. Because I had 29, the years that you 
should be marrying and having a family and raising some 
grandchildren for your - and great grandchildren for 
your mother and grandfather who are here today, you’re 
going to be wasting your time in a prison. And I feel 
sorry for that - for you for that. 


152 


And yet there is no motion to go below the guide- 
lines by the government. In the absence of such a motion, 
I can’t do it. So, therefore, I have no discretion in this 
matter but to impose the sentence that is required by 
what I feel are harsh laws. 


So, for these reasons, it is the sentence and judgment 
of this Court that you be committed to the custody of the 
Attorney General of the United States to be confined in 
an institution to be designated by him for a period of 240 
months and that you shall be given credit thereon for the 
time that you have been in [23] federal custody awaiting 
sentence; and that upon your release from confinement, 
that you shall serve a period of five years of supervised 


release. 


That is not so much a penalty for you as it is to give 
you a probation officer who will help you in the readjust- 
ment process that will be needed. You'll find that the 
probation officer can be your best friend and is there 


specifically to help you. 


During that period of time that you are serving as 
well as serving unsupervised release, | am going to 
impose - because of the large quantities of drugs 
involved in this case, I think some fine is necessary. I 
recognize the fact that there has been a forfeiture of 
almost all of the illicit gains that you achieved. 


And I really think that the explanation of this, Mr. 
Truman, is that here’s a bright young man who was 
competitive, and in the interest of being - achieving on 
the scene, that he saw the easiest place of quick gains and 
advancement was the drug scene. 


153 


And you know, I'll say this. You were good at it. It’s 
too bad. I agree with Mr. Truman, it’s a great shame that 
your energies weren't channeled on Wall Street, because I 
think you would be a great success. 


However, I am - the recommendation is that I fine 
you $25,000, but I think that is unfair and too [24] high. 
I’m going to make it $5,000, so that it’s something that 
you can pay during the time that you are earning money 
in the prison industry business. And when you're on 
supervised release, the supervised release shall be under 
condition that you shall not commit any crimes - federal, 
state or local - and you are prohibited from having fire- 


arms, ammunition, or explosives of any kind from here 
on out. 


Again, you cannot possess any controlled substances 
and shall abide by the standard conditions of supervised 
release. But in addition, you shall participate in programs 
of drug testing and treatment for drugs and alcohol as 
directed by your probation officer, and shall submit to 
mandatory testing on demand. 


And I’m going to impose on you a requirement of 500 
hours of community service. That’s in lieu of the other 
amount of the fine that I have dropped. But I think that 
the 500 hours of community service will help you in your 
adjustment process. You'll be doing something for others. 
And I think that’s a good thing for you to do and con- 


sider. So I’m going to require that in the course of your 
supervised release. 


With that, Mr. Libretti, you are remanded to the 
custody of the marshal and you will have to go with [25] 
him. Good luck. Did you have something further?

UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE DISTRICT OF WYOMING 
(Caption Omitted In Printing) 


ORDER OF FORFEITURE 
(Filed Dec. 23, 1992) 


WHEREAS, in the Indictment in the above-entitled 
case, the plaintiff sought forfeiture to the United States of 
America of specific property of the Defendant, pursuant 
to 21 U.S.C. § 853: 


AND WHEREAS, on October 5, 1992, the Defendant 
agreed to the forfeiture of any right, title and interest as 
to him, as to all such property described hereinafter, more 
specifically described in Count 6 of said Indictment, in 
Bills of Particulars, and in the United States’ motions for 
Restraining Orders against such property. On that date, 
the Defendant entered a plea of guilty to the offense 
listed in Count 6, 21 U.S.C. § 848, and also entered into a 
plea agreement signed and executed by all the parties in 
which the Defendant agreed to forfeit all property; 


AND WHEREAS, by virtue of said stipulations, 
agreements, and pleas of guilty, the United States is now 
entitled to said property, and it should reduce said prop- 
erty to its possession, pursuant to 18 U.S.C. § 848 and 853, 
= Rule 32(b)(2) of the Federal Rules of Criminal -Pro- 

ure.

the United States is required to publish notice of the

manner as the Attorney General may direct. The Govern-

interest in the property that is the subject of the Order o 


Forfeiture as a substitute for published notice as to those 
persons so notified;

alleged interest in the property, shall be signed by the 
petitioner under penalty of perjury, and shall set forth the

supporting the petitioner’s claim and the relief sought.

IMPRISONMENT 


The defendant is hereby committed to the custody of 
the United States Bureau of Prisons to be imprisoned for 
a term of two hundred forty (240) months. Defendant 
shall participate in the Inmate Financial Responsibility 
Program regarding the fine and special assessment 
imposed by this Court.

States Marshal.

SUPERVISED RELEASE 


Upon release from imprisonment, the defendant shall 
be on supervised release for a term of five (5) years. 


While on supervised release, the defendant shall not 
commit another Federal, state or local crime and shall not 
illegally possess a controlled substance. The defendant 
shall comply with the standard conditions that have been 
adopted by this Court (set forth on the following page). If 
this judgment imposes a restitution obligation, it shall be 
a condition of supervised release that the defendant pay 
any such restitution that remains unpaid at the commen- 
cement of the term of supervised release. The defendant 
shall comply with the following additional conditions., 


The defendant shall not use or possess a fire- 
arm, ammunition, dangerous weapons or 
destructive device. Supervised release shall be 
revoked for possession of a firearm. 


168 


Defendant shall not use or possess any illegal 
drugs or controlled substances. Supervised 
release shall be revoked for possession of illegal 
drugs or controlled substances. 


Defendant shall participate in drug and alcohol 
treatment programs as directed by the U.S. Pro- 
bation Office. 


Defendant shall submit to urinalysis testing as 
directed by the U.S. Probation Office. 


Defendant shall contribute 500 hours of commu- 
nity service as directed by the U.S. Probation 
Office. 

Defendant shall pay any remaining balance of 


the $5,000.00 fine imposed by this Court as 
directed by the U.S. Probation Office. 


STANDARD CONDITIONS OF SUPERVISION 


While the defendant is on probation or supervised 
release pursuant to this Judgment: 


1) the defendant shall not leave the judicial district 
without the permission of the Court of. probation 
officer; 

2) the defendant shall report to the probation officer as 
directed by the Court or probation officer and shall 
submit a truthful and complete written report within 
the first five days of each month; 

3) the defendant shall answer truthfully all inquiries by 
the probation officer and follow the instructions of 
the probation officer; 

4) the defendant shall support his or her dependents 
and meet other family responsibilities; 


169 


5) the defendant shall work regularly at a lawful occu- 
pation unless excused by the probation officer for 
schooling, training or other acceptable reasons; 

6) the defendant shall notify the probation officer 
within seventy-two hours of any change in residence 
or employment; 

7) the defendant shall refrain from excessive use of alco- 
hol and shall not purchase, possess, use, distribute, or 
administer any narcotic or other controlled substance, 
or any paraphernalia related to such substances, 
except as prescribed by a physician; 

8) the defendant shall not frequent places where con- 
trolled substances are illegally sold, used, distribd- 
uted, or administered; 

9) the defendant shall not associate with any persons 
engaged in criminal activity, and shall not associate 
with any person convicted of a felony unless granted 
permission to do so by the probation officer; 

10) the defendant shall permit a probation officer to visit 
him or her at any time at home or elsewhere and shal! 
permit confiscation of any contraband observed in 
plain view by the probation officer; 

11) the defendant shall notify the probation officer 
within seventy-two hours of being arrested or ques- 
tioned by a law enforcement officer; 

12) the defendant shall not enter into any agreement to 
act as an informer or a special agent of a law enforce- 
ment agency without the permission of the Court; 

13) as directed by the probation officer, the defendant 
shall notify third parties of risks that may be occa- 
sioned by the defendant's criminal record or personal 
history or characteristics, and shall permit the proba- 
tion officer to make such notifications and to confirm 


i 


170 


the defendant's compliance with such notification 
requirement. 
These conditions are in addition to any other condi- 


tions imposed by this judgment. 


FINE WITH SPECIAL ASSESSMENT 


The defendant shall pay to the United States the sum 
of $5050.00, consisting of a fine of $5000.00 and a special 
assessment of $50.00. 


These amounts are the totals of the fines and assess- 
ments imposed on individual counts as follows: 


Count 6 - Fine $5,000.00; Special Assessment 
$50.00 


This sum shall be paid while defendant is incar- 
cerated and any remaining balance shall be paid 
during the term of supervised release as 
directed by the U.S. Probation Office.

[TRANSCRIPT OF PROCEEDINGS, HEARING 
FEB. 9, 1993] 


* * * 


[13] THE COURT: The question is: Miss 
Woodhouse just informed the court that the United States 
has a pending offer to purchase it, and that the co-owner 
thinks it should be done, the United States thinks it 
should be done, and if it isn’t sold it will be forfeited 
pursuant to the mortgage and nobody will get anything 
out of it. 


So the question is: Shouldn't the sale be consum- 
mated? 


DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: Well, first of all, um, I 
would like to ask if I could be entitled to representation 
here today. 


THE COURT: At this hearing? 
DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: At this hearing. 


THE COURT: As I understand you have filed 
these matters pro se, and Mr. Truman has withdrawn. 


DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: And that’s why I filed 
them pro se, because I could not afford counsel due to my 
indigency. 

THE COURT: These are matters that don’t 
really relate to your criminal suit except incidentally. | am 


not sure if you are entitled to counsel being appointed or 
not. 


DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: Then, I guess, at this 
late [14] date I would like to request appointment of 
counsel under, | believe it is, Title 28 USC 1915(d). 


174 


THE COURT: I will take that under advisement. 
DEFENDANT LIBRETT!t: Thank you. 


THE COURT: In the meantime, tell me what 
your position is on this. 


DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: Well, when I was 
being represented by Mr. Truman I received a letter after 
trial that had been sent before trial from Mr. Cismaru 
saying that I should give him power of attorney for him 
to sell this real estate, but Mr. Truman said that the 
government had some type of hold over it, and I don’t 
know that much about it. 


Did they file some type of injunction; or restraining 
order, or something like that? 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Yes, we did, Your Honor. 


THE COURT: What was done, Miss Wood- 
house? 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Early on in the proceed- 
ings, after indictment, we obtained a restraining order 
prohibiting diminution or waste of the properties. We 
would have agreed to a sale if the other parties had 
agreed to it, but Mr. Libretti did not do so. 


DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: I was never 
approached, Your Honor. 


MS. WOODHOUSE: We had an interest at that 
time [15] of doing that. 


DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: I don’t know what 
she is talking about; I didn’t agree to any sale, | am not 


175 


aware of that ever coming up. This is the first I have 
heard about a sale of the condominium. 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Your Honor, it is not his 
condominium anymore. You did enter an order of forfei- 
ture as to 50 percent of that condominium in December, 
and we are simply trying to make sure we can have a sale 
of that in agreement with the other 50 percent owner. 


DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: I have to dispute that 
again, Your Honor. I wasn’t aware that that condominium 
was included in any forfeiture. There certainly are no 


proceeds derived directly or indirectly from a violation of 
Title 21. 


THE COURT: The order of forfeiture has been 
entered in this case. I'm sure you have received a copy of 
it. 

DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: I did not get a copy of 
that order of forfeiture until I filed my appeal, and much 


after that, and I had never seen it before. And I had not 
seen it at the time of the plea agreement. 


THE COURT: You have seen it? 


DEFENDANT LIBRETTI: Yes. Craig Truman 
mailed me a copy well after the sentencing even. I had 
not seen that [16] at the time I entered the plea agrce- 
ment. 


THE COURT: I have just got the last volume of 
your file in front of me, and I am not sure if that contains 
the order or not. 


MS. WOODHOUSE: It was filed on the 23rd of 
December, Your Honor. 


=—_. 


176 


THE COURT: Let me get up to December. 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Your Honor, we dealt with 
this matter of stay, and I believe Mr. Libretti is not enti- 
tled to a stay. 


THE COURT: Yes, I have a copy of the order of 
forfeiture that was filed December 23rd, 1992, and | 
remember that order, which does forfeit the various items 
of property that are listed therein. 

Was this condominium one of those items? 


— 


MS. WOODHOUSE: Yes, it is, Your Honor. I 
believe it is item (B). 


THE -OURT: Item (B)? 
MS. WOODHOUSE: Yes. 


THE COURT: Yes, that’s the one that’s owned. 


jointly with Mr. Cismaru.

that Mr. Libretti sold during the conspiracy and the 
amounts of money that he earned. You said earlier that it 
was probably at least one million dollars? 


A. From the time that - well, just from the records 
that we seized from the first search in January of 1991 
from all the accounts that he had, the amounts that we 
saw funneling through those accounts was over $800,000. 
And that was just for the time period - and that involved 
very few of the accounts. 


Now, from 1981 to 1991 our best estimate of his drug 
trafficking is that he had profited in excess of a million 
dollars, and that was a conservative estimate after our 
discussion with you even recently. 


[97] Q. What quantity of drugs was he selling? 


A. Kilogram quantities of cocaine purchased from 
between 20 to $30,000, each cut, sold, distributed over the 
entire life of the conspiracy. 


Now, not so much in the great amounts in the earlier 
years, but certainly as time went on. 


Q. And total kilograms of cocaine that you believe - 


A. Would be over 50 kilograms. We had an estimate 
of 76 kilograms from the codefendants. At one time we 


have reduced that conservatively to a 60 kilograms esti- 
mate. ; 


238 


Q. And a profit on each kilogram of? 
A. I haven't calculated the specific kilograms. 


Q. As far as narcotic profits, can you give us a 
summary of that? 


A. The witnesses and the codefendants were not 
certain on the amounts of marijuana that had been sold. 
They saw amounts totaling what they believe to be 600 
pounds at a time, and there was reportedly customers 
purchasing 200 pounds at a time, customers from back 


east. 


Now, in their best estimates, he at no time would 
have gone through more than a ton of marijuana. And 
that was due in part because the product — this was in the 
earlier years, but the product was bulky, smelled, and 
that’s the reason they shifted - Joe Libretti’s organization 
shifted from marijuana to [98] cocaine was because of the 
bulkiness was such that it required storage lockers, it 
smelled, and the profits weren't as great as they were in 
cocaine sales. 


Q. So your conservative estimate of the actual 
profits that the defendant earned between 1982 and 1991, 
is what? 


A. A million dollars. In excess of a million dollars.

The first method, the lowest intermediate [131] bal- 
ance method has been described as the drugs in, last out 


rule. Under this rule the tainted $10,000 remains balance 
in the account until the time when the account falls below 
this amount. As long as the account balance remains 
above $10,000, all deposits and withdrawals are immune 
‘rom seizure. It is the account that if considered retrace- 
able, held proceeds of $10,000.

the account activity from the previous example -

THE WITNESS: Using the account activity from 
the previous example, we have three $10,000 deposits of 
which one is tainted; one third is traceable proceeds 
subject to seizure. As before, the check is written against 
the account for the purchase of 18 machine guns for 
$10,000. Under the averaging rule, a prorata share of each 
withdrawal is tainted. Since one third of the account was 
illegal proceeds at the time of purchase, one third of 
every purchase is subject to seizure in addition to one 
third of the account. 


[135] So, since 18 machine guns were bought at this 
point, six machines guns are subject to seizure, one third 
times 18 and one third of the $20,000 in the account is 
subject to seizure, approximately 6,000 - $6,666.67. 


Any deposits into this account would decrease the 
percentage of tainted funds in the account. But the dollar 
amount of tainted funds would still be one third of 
$20,000 or $6,666.67. 


If another $10,000 check is written against the 
account for the purchase of 18 machine guns, bringing 
the account balance down to 10,000 again, six of these 
machine guns are subject to seizure, one third of the 
purchase, and one third of the account balance or $3,333 
is subject to seizure. 


And the last method is - doesn’t really have a name, 
it’s just known as drugs in, first out. And under this 
method, the first purchase would be the first tainted 
money out of the account. And thus the first purchase of 


273 


ten machine guns — of 18 machine guns for $10,000 would 
be subject to seizure while the rest of the account is 
immune.

“There is a plausible argument, as SONAL suggests, 
for cumulative forfeiture of all assets exchanged for 
drugs or drug proceeds. For example, if $100 from the 
sale of drugs is exchanged for a watch, both the $100 in 
the hands of the watch seller and the watch in the hands 
of the drug seller are vulnerable to traceable proceeds, 
and the government may well be entitled to forfeit: both. 
However, cumulative forfeiture is not permissible simply 
because available accounting rules afford the government 
more than one route in tracing forfeitable proceeds. For 
example, if $100 from the sale of drugs is deposited in an 
account funded with untainted money, $100 in the 
account and each $100 withdrawal are all vulnerable to 
forfeiture, but the government can obtain only a single 
forfeiture of $100.”

ORDER AMENDING DECEMBER 23, 1992 
FORFEITURE ORDER 


(Filed Apr. 2, 1993) 


This matter comes before the Court on March 26, 
1993. The Court, having considered the materials on file 
both in support of and in opposition to the motions, 
having considered the arguments of counsel, and being 
fully advised in the premises, FINDS and ORDERS as 


follows: 


BACKGROUND 


Joseph Libretti (“Libretti”) pleaded guilty to Count 6 
of the superseding indictment, which charged him with 
engaging in a continuing criminal enterprise in violation 
of 21 U.S.C. § 848. On December 23, 1992, Libretti was 
sentenced to 240 months incarceration and was fined 
$5,000. Certain assets owned by Libretti were seized by 
the government pursuant to an order entered by this 
Court on December 23, 1992, in accordance with the 
criminal forfeiture statute, 21 U.S.C. § 853(a) (1992), 


which provides: 


(a) Property subject to criminal forfeiture 
Any person convicted of a violation of this sub- 
chapter . . . punishable by imprisonment for more 
than one year shall forfeit to the United States, 
irrespective of any provision of State law - 


299 


(1) any property constituting, or derived 
from, any proceeds the person obtained, 
directly or indirectly, as the result of such 
violation; 

(2) any of the person’s property used, or 
intended to be used, in any manner or part, 
to commit, or to facilitate the commission 
of, such violation; and 

(3) in the case of a person convicted of 
engaging in a continuing criminal enter- 
prise in violation of section 848 of this title, 
the person shall forfeit, in addition to any 
property described in paragraph (1) or (2), 
any of his interest in, claims against, and 
property or contractual rights affording a 
source of control over, the continuing crimi- 
nal enterprise. 

Libretti filed a motion for stay of execution of order 
of criminal forfeiture, and to amend the order of criminal 
forfeiture. In addition, Joseph Libretti Sr. (Libretti’s 
father) and Claudia Porter filed motions for return of 
property pursuant to 21 U.S.C. 853(n)(6)(A) and (B), 
which provides: 


(n) Third party interest 

(6) If, after the hearing, the court determines 
that the petitioner has established by a prepon- 
derance of the evidence that - 


(A) the petitioner has a legal right, title, or inter- 
est in the property, and such right, title, or inter- 
est renders the order of forfeiture invalid in 
whole or in part because the right, title, or inter- 
est was vested in the petitioner rather than the 
defendant or was superior to any right, title, or 


300 


i t of the defendant at the time of the com- 
pemares of the acts which gave rise to the forfei- 
ture of the property under this section; or (B) 
The petitioner is a bona fide purchaser for value 
of the right, title, or interest in the property and 
was at the time of purchase reasonably without 
cause to believe that the property was subject to 
forfeiture under this section; 


the court shall amend the order of forfeiture in 
accordance with its determination. (emphasis 


added). 


On February 12, 1993, the Court ordered the govern- 
ment to maintain the status quo on all forfeited property 
pending this hearing. The Court however, made an 
exception allowing the government to sell two condomin- 
iums located at 9430 and 9328 West Ontario Drive, Lit- 
tleton, Colorado. The Court further ordered the 
government to notify all parties known to be claiming an 
interest in the forfeited property of this hearing. 


DISCUSSION 
Third Party Interests 


Notice of forfeiture was published three times from 
January 6, 1993 to January 21, 1993, pursuant to 21 U.S.C. 
§ 853(n) in the following newspapers: (1) Green River 
Star, Green River, Wyoming; (2) Chicago Tribune, Chi- 
cago, Illinois; (3) Kemmerer Gazette, Kemmerer, —— 
ming; (4) Wyoming State Tribune, Cheyenne, Wyoming; 
and (5) The Rocky Mountain News, Denver, Colorado. In 
addition, the following people were notified of this hear- 
ing by a letter dated February 19, 1993, sent by certified 
mail return receipt requested: Mr. and Mrs. Edward Zak, 


301 


Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Libretti Sr., AT&T Universal Card, 


Heckler & Koch Inc., Claudia Porter, and Group Indus- 
tries. 


The United States, in seeking forfeiture, “must estab- 
lish probable cause to believe that a substantial connec- 
tion exists between the property to be forfeited and an 
illegal exchange of a controlled substance.” U.S. v. One 
Single Family Residence Located at 15603 85th Avenue North, 
Lake Park, Palm Beach County, Florida, 933 F.2d 976, 979 
(11th Cir. 1991). “Once the government has met its bur- 
den of showing probable cause to institute the forfeiture 
action, the burden then shifts to the claimant to show by 
a preponderance of the evidence that the property was 
not subject to forfeiture.” United States v. Twenty-Two 
Thousand, Two Hundred Eighty Seven Dollars ($22,287.00), 
United States Currency, 709 F.2d 442, 446 (6th Cir. 1983); 
see also United States v. One Parcel of Real Property With 
Buildings, Appurtenances, and Improvements, Known as Plat 
20, Lot 17, Great Harbor Neck, New Shoreham, Rhode Island, 
960 F.2d 200, 204 (1st Cir. 1992). 


The criminal forfeiture statutes protect innocent 
owners of forfeited property. This protection is not lim- 
ited to bona fide purchasers. United States v. A Parcel of 
Land, Buildings, Appurtenances and Improvements, Known as 


92 Buena Vista Avenue, Rumson, New Jersey, 113 S. Ct. 1116, 
1134 (1993). 


21 U.S.C. § 881(a)(6) provides that: 


[N]o property shall be forfeited . . . to the extent 
of the interest of an owner, by reason of any act 
or omission established by that owner to have 


302 


been committed or omitted without the knowl- 
edge or consent of that owner. 


This clause “exempts certain proceeds - proceeds owned 
by one unaware of their criminal course from forfeiture.” 
Buena Vista at 1136. This is known as the “innocent 
owner” defense. Thus, property is not forfeitable unless 
(1) it can be traced to illegal sales of controlled sub- 
stances; and (2) there is no innocent owner. 


Mrs. Joseph Libretti (defendant's mother) and Clau- 
dia Porter appeared at the hearing to assert their interests 


in certain forfeited property. 


(1) $1,333.45 to Claudia Porter 


In December of 1990, Porter used a power of attorney 
from Libretti to transfer $9,333.45 from Libretti’s 
Wyochem account to her personal account in State Bank 
of Green River. Porter sent $8,000 from this account to 
Legal Research in Chicago, Illinois for Libretti’s defense 
costs. The remaining $1,333.45 was frozen, and subse- 
quently taken by the government pursuant to the Decem- 
ber 23, 1992 forfeiture order. 


Porter argues that she should be able to keep the 
$1,333.45 as compensation for services rendered and 
expenditures made in caring for Libretti’s dogs. Porter 
asserts that she thought these funds were legitimate. The 
government counters that the funds were “tainted,” and 


that Porter knew it. 


Libretti’s testimony at the hearing established that at 
least a portion of the Wyochem account consisted of 
tainted funds, however, it is unclear what percentage. 


303 


The government argues that becau i 

se a portion of the 
funds can be tied to illegal drug sales, that the full 
amount in the account should be forfeited.’ 


In certain circumstances, even though 
, only part of a 
person's property is tainted, the whole property may be 
subject to forfeiture. Section 853(a)(2) provides that “any 
of the person’s property used, or intended to be used, in

! stitute asset” th

per 8. and the total value of the assets forfeited is signif 
cantly less, the government argues that all of the funds in the 
account should be forfeited as “substitue assets,” even th 
only a portion is tainted. seni 
21 U.S.C. § 853(p) provides: 
(p) Forfeiture of substitute property

—_— - ——_ 


any manner or part, to com 
mission of, such violation” shall be forfeited. (emphasis 


added). The Tenth Circuit interpreted § 853(a)(2) in 


304 


mit, or to facilitate the com- 


United States v. Harris, 903 F.2d 770, 777 (10th Cir. 1990). 
In that case, the Tenth Circuit held that $413,493 in cash, 
which was found in a vehicle along with three-quarters of 
a pound of marijuana and other drug paraphernalia, was 
properly forfeited because the $413,493 was used or was 
intended to be used to facilitate the illegal activity. Id. 


However, this case is different. The government has 
not argued that the funds in Libretti’s Wyochem account 
were used directly to facilitate an illegal activity. There- 
fore, the analysis of § 853(a)(2) in Harris is not directly in 
point. Rather, the proper focus is on § 853(a)(1) and 
§ 881(a)(6). Section 853(a)(1) provides that “any property 
constituting, or derived from, any proceeds the person 
obtained, directly or indirectly, as the result of such viola- 
tion” shall be forfeited. Section 881(a)(6) provides that: 


All moneys, negotiable instruments, securities, 
or other things of value furnished or intended to 


be furnished by any person in exchange for a 
controlled substance in violation of this sub- 
chapter, all proceeds traceable to such 
exchange . . . [shall be subject to forfeiture]. 


(emphasis added). 


The key is that under these provisions, only property 
that is traceable to the illegal transaction is forfeitable. 
United States v. One 1980 Rolls Royce, 905 F.2d 89, 91-92 
(5th Cir. 1990) (“We conclude that a court should not 
grant summary judgment and permit the complete forfei- 
ture of all properties when there is evidence that the 
properties were purchased at least in part with legitimate 


305 


funds. Though the burden will be on the claimant h 
should be permitted to establish what portions of os 
properties were purchased with legitimate funds.”); 
United States v. Banco Cafetero Panama, 797 F.2d 1154 (2d 
Cir. 1986); United States v. Certain Real Property Located at 
2323 Charms Road, Milford Township, 726 F. Supp. 164 
169-70 (E.D. Mich. 1989); United States v. One Parcel g Real 
Property With Buildings, 705 F. Supp. 710 (D. R.I. 1989) 
vacated on other grounds by 970 F.2d 370 (1st Cir. 1990) 


This Court agrees with the Fif ircui 
i a a ifth Circuit, when it 


[T]he interest acquired as a result o 

payments made with the proceeds of a. 
actions should be forfeitable. We do not believe 
however, that forfeitability spreads like a dis- 
ease from one infected mortgage payment to the 
entire interest in the property acquired prior to 
the payment. After all, only the actual proceeds 
of drug transactions are forfeitable. Unless sec- 
tion 881(a)(6) deprives persons accused of deal- 
ing drugs of the right to own any property, the 
existence of an undivided interest in a felon’s 
property which constitutes proceeds cannot 
mean that his entire property is proceeds. 


One 1980 Rolls Royce 905 F.2d at 90 quoting Real Propert 

Located at 2323 Charms Rd. 726 F. Supp. at 169-79. Appl : 
ing this reasoning to the instant case, the Court holds ad 
the mere fact that a portion of Libretti’s Wyochem 
account is tainted, does not mean that the entire account 
is forfeitable. Banco Cafetero Panama 797 F.2d at 1159. 


it is unclear from the testimony at this hearing what 
portion of Libretti’s Wyochem account can be traced to an 


inn 


306 


illegal transaction. Without this information, it is also 
unclear what portion of the $1,333.45, which was been 
claimed by Porter, is traceable to an illegal transaction. If 
none of the money is traceable to an illegal transaction, 
the $1,333.45 should be remitted to Porter. Even if all, or a 
portion of the $1,333.45 can be traced to an illegal trans- 
action, Porter may still be entitled to the full amount if 
she can show by a preponderance of the evidence that she 
is an innocent owner. 21 U.S.C. § 881(a)(6). 


The Court orders that a hearing be held before the 
magistrate to make findings of fact as to the source of 
funds in the Wyochem account. The Court will reserve 
ruling on the disposition of the $1,333.45 claim made by 


Porter. 


(2) Two Piece Diamond Engagement Ring to Claudia 
Porter 

In October of 1990, Libretti gave Porter a diamond 
ring set valued at $2,850. Testimony showed that Libretti 
and Porter had an intimate relationship from November 
of 1989 through December of 1991. 


After hearing testimony, the Court finds that the 
diamond ring set was purchased with legitimate funds. 
The Court also finds that Porter is an innocent owner of 
the diamond ring set, having no knowledge that any 
funds traceable to illegal acts were used to purchase the 


set.

(5) Other Third Party Claims 


Although proper notice was given, no other parties 
appeared at the hearing to assert their third party inter- 
ests pursuant to 21 U.S.C. § 853(n). The Court holds that 
any third party interests that were not asserted at the 
March 26, 1993 hearing are waived. 


Libretti’s Interests 


On October 5, 1992, Libretti entered into a plea agree- 
ment in which he agreed to forfeit all of the property 
listed in the Motion for Entry of Forfeiture Order filed by 
the government on December 23, 1992, pursuant to 21 
U.S.C. § 853. In accordance with this plea agreement, the 
Court entered an Order of Forfeiture, which was filed on 
December 23, 1992. Subsequently, Libretti asserted inter- 
ests in the forfeited property. The government argues that 
Libretti already agreed to forfeit specific property in the 
plea agreement, and therefore he has no right to claim an 
interest in that property. 


However, after further review of the plea agreement 
and the December 23, 1992 Order of Forfeiture, the Court 
has determined that it may be unjust to enforce the 
specific forfeiture provisions in the plea agreement. For- 
feiture statutes do not mandate forfeiture of property 
purchased with legitimate funds. United States v. One 1980 
Rolls Royce, VIN # SRL 390955, 905 F.2d 89, 90 (Sth Cir. 
1990) (“We do not believe . . . that forfeitability spreads 
like a disease... . ”). 


It is also important to note that “a defendant's con- 
cession in a plea agreement to the forfeiture of his prop- 
erty ‘does not make it so.’ ” United States v. Boatner, 966 
F.2d 1575, 1581 (11th Cir. 1992) citing United States v. 
Reckmeyer, 786 F.2d 1216, 1222 (4th Cir. 1986); see also 
United States v. Roberts, 749 F.2d 404, 409 (7th Cir. 1984) 
cert. denied 479 U.S. 850 (1986) (“The mere fact that the 
defendant has agreed an item is forfeitable in a plea 
agreement, does not make it so.”). 


ee en i i es 


ae OTe eee a ae 


——--;-- - - — - 


/ 
| 
; 
| 
. 
. 


310 


Finally, this Court is not bound to accept a og 
ture provisions in a plea agreement. Fev. R. am. P. 
11(e)(4). Indeed, the Court may have some obligation in 


relation to the plea agreement to provide safeguards to 
“insure the defendant what is reasonably due in the 
circumstances.” United States v. Baldacchino, 762 F.2d 170 
(1st Cir. 1985); Santobello v. New York, 404 U.S. 257 (1971).

LOGAN, Circuit Judge. 


Defendant Joseph V. Libretti, Jr. appeals from an 
order of criminal forfeiture. He contends that (1) the 
district court erred in not making factual findings as to 
the forfeitability of assets; (2) he was not advised of and 
did not waive his right to a jury trial on the forfeiture 


* Honorable David L. Russell, District Judge, United States Dis- 
trict Court for the Western District of Oklahoma, sitting by 
designation. 


314 


issues; and (3) even if his assets were properly forfeited, 
the forfeiture order violated the Excessive Fines Clause of 
the Eighth Amendment. We exercise jurisdiction under 18 
U.S.C. § 3742(a) and affirm the order of forfeiture.' 


A grand jury returned a superseding indictment 
against defendant containing eleven counts of various 
drug, firearms, and money laundering violations, includ- 
ing continuing criminal enterprise (CCE), 21 U.S.C. § 848. 
After one week of trial, defendant entered into a plea 
agreement. Pursuant to that agreement, he pleaded guilty 
to the CCE count and to forfeiture of “all known assets as 
prescribed in 21 U.S.C. § 853 and assets which are discov- 
ered at any later time up to $1,500,000.” I R. tab 210 at 2. 
The plea agreement further provided that defendant 


would forfeit 


his right, title, and interest in all of his assets 
_ including, but not limited to: all real estate; 
all personal property, including guns, the com- 
uter, and every other item now in the posses- 
sion of the United States; all bank accounts, 
investments, retirement accounts, cash, cashier's 
checks, travelers checks, and funds of any kind. 


Id. at 3. In exchange for defendant's plea agreement the 
government agreed to recommend the minimum sentence 
applicable to a CCE conviction, twenty years imprison- 
ment, and not to pursue other criminal charges against 


him.

ordered submitted without oral argument.

II 


Defendant argues that the district court erred in 
ordering forfeiture of the nonforfeitable property in light 
of his objection at the sentencing and the court’s own 
post-judgment rulings. He alleges that factual findings 
must be made establishing that the assets to be forfeited 
are part of a continuing series of offenses. Defendant 
maintains that the tria! testimony and change of plea and 
sentencing hearings show that there was not a sufficient 
nexus to support a forfeiture of accounts containing legit- 
imate earnings such as his employees savings plan, IRA 
accounts, and bank accounts containing his paychecks. 


Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 11(f) provides 
that “[nJotwithstanding the acceptance of a plea of guilty, 
the court should not enter a judgment upon such plea 
without making such inquiry as shall satisfy it that there 
is a factual basis for the plea.”5 The government contends 
that Rule 11(f) applies only to the guilty plea and imposes 


° Defendant also contends that Fed.R.Crim.P

curacy in the presentence report. Because the district court did

320 


no duty on the district court to ensure a sufficient factual 
basis exists to support the stipulated forfeiture in the plea 
agreement. Whether Rule 11(f) applies to a stipulated 
forfeiture in a plea agreement is an issue of first impres- 
sion in this circuit. Four circuits have considered this 


issue and have reached divergent conclusions. 


In United States v. Roberts, 749 F.2d 404, 409 (7th 
Cir.1984) (a RICO case), cert. denied, 470 U.S. 1058, 105 
S.Ct. 1770, 84 L.Ed.2d 830 (1985),* the Seventh Circuit 
held that Rule 11(f) applies to forfeitures included as part 
of a plea agreement just as it applies to the guilty plea. 
“The mere fact that the defendant has agreed that an item 
is forfeitable, in a plea agreement, does not make it so; 
the trial court must ascertain whether it [is forfeitable].” 
Id. “A defendant’s waiver of his right to trial cannot be 
said to have a factual basis, where a forfeiture of property 
is involved, unless the property is in fact subject to forfei- 
ture.” Id. at 410. 


In United States v. Reckmeyer, 786 F.2d 1216 (4th Cir.), 
cert. denied, 479 U.S. 850, 107 S.Ct. 177, 93 L.Ed.2d 113 
(1986), the Fourth Circuit agreed with the reasoning in 
Roberts that a defendant’s mere agreement to forfeit an 
item in the plea agreement does not make the item forfei- 
table. The court held that Rule 11(f) “requires the district 


court to inquire beyond the agreement to determine if 
there is a factual basis for the forfeiture of the assets.” Id.

Cir.), cert. denied, 493 U.S. 849, 876, 110 S.Ct. 146, 213, 107 


L.Ed.2d 104, 166 (1989).

The Fifth Circuit took a more lenient approach in 
United States v. Bachynsky, 949 F.2d at 722, 730 (5th 
Cir.1991), cert. denied, __ U.S. __, 113 S.Ct. 150, 121 
L.Ed.2d 101 (1992), when it held “that the sequivement of 
Rule 11(f) that the factual basis for a plea be established 
at ve 4 plea hearing does not apply to an order of forfei- 
ture.” It determined the district court’s order of forfeiture 
would be upheld if the record provided a factual basis for 
forfeiture. Id. (citing United States v. Crumibley, 872 F.2d 
975, 977 (11th Cir. 1989)). Although the court acknow!l-

Because the defendant in Bachynsky had never objected at

Fifth Circuit concluded that the defendant had not estab-

The Eleventh Circuit rejected the analysis of Reck- 
meyer and Roberts. United States v. Boatner, 966 F.2d 1575,

required under Rule 11 to determine whether there is a

provision is “not a plea to a substami’e charge, but a

defendant's plea,” Rule 11(f) only applies to the guilty 
plea. Id. Because the defendant in Boatner agreed to forfeit 
property as a result of a bargained for exchange between 
the parties, the court affirmed the order of forfeiture. 


We agree with the holding and reasoning in Boatner. 
The parties both acknowledge that criminal forfeiture 
under § 853 is a part of the sentence, not a part of the 
substantive offense. The courts in Roberts, Reckmeyer, and 
Bachynsky fail to note this distinction. We question 
whether the Seventh Circuit would continue to apply the 
holding in Roberts in light of its more recent determina- 
tions that forfeiture is not an element of the offense but 
rather is punishment for an offense when a conviction 
has been entered. See United States v. Simone, 931 F.2d 
1186, 1199 (7th Cir.), cert. denied, _. U.S. ___ 112 S.Ct. 
584, 116 L.Ed.2d 609 (1991); United States v. Herrero, 893 
F.2d 1512, 1541 (7th Cir.), cert. denied, 496 U.S. 927, 110 
S.Ct. 2623, 110 L.Ed.2d 644 (1990). 


In addition, it is settled law that a lawful plea agree- 
ment is enforceable. United States. v. Khan, 835 F.2d 749, 
752 (10th Cir.1987), cert. denied, 487 U.S. 1222, 108 S.Ct. 


is satisfied if the entire record provides a factual basis for the 
forfeiture. Id. at 976. Boatner, however, essentially overrules 
Crumbley. See United States v. Herndon, 982 F.2d 1411, 1417 (10th 


Cir. 1992).

defendant and his attorney may arrive at a bargain “that 


lawful, a defendant should be bound by its terms. United 


Defendant argues that the plea agreement and, there- 
fore, his guilty plea, were not lawful and should not be 
enforced because he agreed to forfeit only the assets 
listed in the indictment or only property directly associ- 
ated with the drug transactions. He contends that the 
plea agreement is ambiguous and vague and he would 
not have knowingly and voluntarily agreed to forfeit all 
of his property. 


The voluntariness of a guilty plea is a tion 
federal law subject to de novo review. tient feme . 
Rhodes, 913 F.2d 839, 843 (10th Cir.1990), cert. denied, 498 
U.S. 1122, 111 S.Ct. 1079, 112 L.Ed.2d 1184 (1991). A 
guilty plea must be knowing and voluntary. Santobello v 
New York, 404 U.S. 257, 261, 92 S.Ct. 495, 498, 30 L.Ed.2d 
427 (1971); United States v. Elias, 937 F.2d 1514, 1517 (10th 
Cir.1991). A defendant's decision to plead guilty must be 
deliberate and intelligent and chosen from available alter- 
natives. Rhodes, 913 F.2d at 843. “[T]o determine whether 
a plea is voluntary, a court must assess whether the 
defendant fully understood the consequences of the 
plea.” United States v. Williams, 919 F.2d 1451, 1456 (10th 
Cir.1990), cert. denied, 499 U.S. 968, 111 S.Ct. 1604, 113 
L.Ed.2d 667 (1991). The defendant's attorney “and the 


324 


court have a duty to apprise the defendant of the const 
quences of the plea and ensure that it is voluntary. ;, 
Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 11(c)(1) requires that 
a district court must, among other things, ensure the 
defendant understands the nature of the charge and 
mandatory minimum and maximum penalties. Elias, 937 
F.2d at 1517. 


In this case, the district court thoroughly assessed 
whether defendant understood the consequences of the 
guilty plea. The court informed him that a propes'y 
described in the CCE count of the indictment cou 
forfeited. Il R. 7. Defendant only questioned the extent to 
which the $1,500,000 limit on forfeiture applied to future 
legitimate earnings. His attorney and the district court 
agreed that the $1,500,000 limit applied only to seeps 
of drug transactions and not to legitimate ose phd 
ings. Id. at 3-9. Thereafter, defendant stated that 
signed, read, and understood the plea agreement, a 
that he had no questions about it. Id. at 19-20. He further 
stated that he understood the consequences of his ples 
including that all of his property owned by reason ~d 
drug transaction could be forfeited. Id. at 21. adie 
conceded that he was pleading guilty because of the tria 
testimony received thus far. Id. at 22. 


Nothing in the record indicates defendant's plea 
would have been different if he had been questioned 
further about forfeitability of his assets. He intended to 
forfeit all of his property without requiring the govern- 
ment to prove the assets were forfeitable. The plea agree” 
ment requires forfeiture pursuant to § 853, wi ic 
includes forfeitable assets under § 853(a) and substitute 


325 


assets under § 853(p). See also 21 U.S.C. § 853(0) (§ 853 is 
to be liberally construed). 


Further, defendant received a favorable plea agree- 
ment. In exchange for forfeiting all of his property, the 
government recommended the minimum sentence of 
twenty years imprisonment and agreed to drop all other 
counts in the indictment. One of those counts charged 
him with use of firearms with silencers in drug trafficking 
which mandates, upon conviction, a thirty-year sentence 
consecutive to the sentence on the underlying drug 
offense. Defendant entered into the plea agreement after 
the government presented overwhelming evidence of his 
guilt. He now seeks the benefit of that bargain, but only 
to the extent it favors him. We will not require the gov- 
ernment to undergo a fact-finding hearing and forego a 
substantial benefit of its bargain. 


We hold that defendant is bound by the terms of the 
plea agreement, see Alexander, 869 F.2d at 94, in which he 
unambiguously forfeited all of his property. The district 
court’s extraneous statements that defendant was only 
forfeiting assets listed in the CCE count of the indictment 
or from his drug trade do not establish that defendant did 
not knowingly and voluntarily forfeit all of his property. 
Rather, defendant agreed to forfeit all of his assets, 
including some that on their face had no connection with 
his drug dealings - e.g., “all bank accounts, investments, 
retirement accounts,” I R. tab 210 at 3. Nothing in the 
agreement limits the forfeiture to those assets with an 
established factual basis for forfeiture. Defendant is also 
not limited to forfeiting only those assets directly related 
to the CCE conviction. Accordingly, the district court 
properly ordered on December 23, 1992, the forfeiture of 


326 


all of defendant's assets* based on defendant's written 
agreement and knowing and voluntary ratification of that 
agreement at the change of plea hearing. 


Ill 


Defendant argues that he was not advised of and did 
not waive his right to a jury trial on forfeiture issues. 
Specifically, defendant contends he did not sign a written 
waiver of his right to a jury trial and there was no 
colloquy with the court during the plea or sentencing 
hearing regarding his right to or waiver of a trial on 
forfeitability of assets. . 


“The Sixth Amendment never has been thought to 
guarantee a right to a jury determination [on punish- 
ment].” Spaziano v. Florida, 468 U.S. 447, 459, 104 S.Ct. 
3154, 3161, 82 L.Ed.2d 340 (1984) (right to jury trial does 
not include right to jury determination whether to impose 
capital sentence). This suggests that defendant does not 
have a constitutional right to a jury trial at the forfeiture 
stage of these proceedings; the circuits, however, would 
apparently split on the issue of a constitutional right to a 
jury trial on forfeiture. Compare United States v. Robinson, 
8 F.3d 418, 420 (7th Cir.1993) (noting in dicta that a 


® Of course, defendant could only agree to forfeit assets 
over which he had a power of disposition. Thus, if he had an 
interest in a profit sharing or pension plan established by his 
corporate employer that was inalienable by law, or if he was 
beneficiary of a valid spendthrift trust created by another per- 
son, these interests would be excluded from forfeiture in the 
same manner as assets in defendant’s possession owned by a 


third party. 


327 


defendant does not have a constitutional right to a 
determination on forfeiture issues, but piivatenaen 
the right to such a determination under Fed.R.Crim.P 
31(e)) with United States v. Garrett, 727 F.2d 1003, 1012 & a. 
6 (11th Cir.1984) (holding that a defendant has , constitu- 
tional right to a jury trial in a 21 U.S.C. § 848 proceeding 
to determine if an asset is forfeitable, after a jury trial as 
to guilt), affirmed on other grounds, 471 U.S. 773, 105 S.Ct. 
2407, 85 L.Ed.2d 764 (1985). We need not decide that issue 
because constitutional rights can be waived, and because 
a defendant does have a statutory right, pursuant to 
Fed.R.Crim.P. 31(e), to have a jury determine which of 
his assets may be forfeited. Robinson, 8 F.3d at 420. Thus, 
we must determine whether defendant waived his right 
to a jury trial. “[A] defendant’s waiver of his statutory 
right to have a jury determine which portion of his prop- 
erty is subject to forfeiture is only valid if knowingly and 
voluntarily made.” Id. at 421. A defendant has the burden 
of proving that he did not exercise a valid waiver of his 
right. Id. at 422. 


A valid waiver of the right to a jury trial depends on 
unique facts of each case. See id. At the plea hearing, the 
district court advised defendant that he could continue 
with the jury trial that had already started. The district 
court told defendant that if he pleaded guilty pursuant to 
the plea agreement, he would give up his right to a jury 
trial and would be sentenced. Defendant stated that he 


* Rule 31(e) provides that “{iJf the indictmen 
t...a 
that an interest or property is subject to criminal forfeiture, a 
special verdict shall be returned as to the extent of the interest 
or property subject to forfeiture if any.” 


328 


understood his rights and chose to plead guilty. Cf. United 
States v. Jenkins, 904 F.2d 549, 558-59 (10th Cir.) (at trial, 
defendant and his counsel have responsibility to make 
trial court aware of desire to testify regarding forfeiture; 
if no request is made, court and government can assume 
that evidence of guilt and forfeiture may be heard 
together), cert. denied, 498 U.S. 962, 111 S.Ct. 395, 112 
L.Ed.2d 404 (1990). The district court did not specifically 
indicate that defendant's guilty plea negated a jury trial 
on any forfeiture issues. But there is no evidence at that 
hearing that defendant wanted a jury trial on the forfei- 
ture issue, or thought he was going to have one, The plea 
agreement itself waived the right to a jury trial without 
specific mention of forfeiture. There is no evidence in the 
record that the government would have agreed to a plea 
agreement that settled only guilt and required a jury trial 
on the assets to be forfeited. We hold that specific refer- 
ence to a jury trial on forfeiture issues was not necessary 
in light of the unambiguous plea agreement and defen- 
dant’s knowing and voluntary plea. Under these circum- 
stances we conclude defendant waived his right to a jury 


trial. 


IV 


Defendant finally argues that even if all of his assets 
were correctly forfeited the forfeiture order violates the 
Excessive Fines Clause of the Eighth Amendment. The 
Supreme Court has determined that forfeiture may be 
analyzed under the Excessive Fines Clause. Alexander v. 
United States, __ US. _. —~ 113 S.Ct. 2766, 2776, 125 
L.Ed.2d 441 (1993) (criminal forfeiture under 18 U.S.C. 
§ 1963(a)); see also United States v. Bieri, 21 F.3d 819, 823 


329 


n.2 (8th Cir.1994) (excessive fines analysis applies to crim- 
inal forfeiture under § 853(a)). The Court, however, has 
not established a test to determine when a forfeiture is 
excessive. Alexander, __ U.S. at __, 113 S.Ct. at 2776; 
Austin v. United States, __ U.S. __, __, 113 S.Ct. 2801, 
2812, 125 L.Ed.2d 488 (1993). Rather, courts must consider 
all factors when evaluating whether a forfeiture is uncon- 
stitutionally excessive. Bieri, 21 F.3d at 824 (citing Alex- 
ander, __ U.S. at __, 113 S.Ct. at 2776). 


We need not decide when a forfeiture might violate 
the Excessive Fines Clause, because the forfeiture in this 
case was not excessive. See United States v. Certain Real 
Property & Premises Known as 38 Whalers Cove Drive, Baby- 
lon, NY, 954 F.2d 29, 39 (2d Cir.) (civil forfeiture), cert. 
denied, __ U.S. __, 113 S.Ct. 55, 121 L.Ed.2d 24 (1992). A 
fine of up to $2,000,000 and forfeiture are permitted for a 
CCE offense. 21 U.S.C. § 848(a); U.S.S.G. § 5E1.2(c)(3), (4). 
Defendant was fined only $5,000. The plea agreement 
stated that property up to $1,500,000 could be forfeited; 
yet the actual value of the property forfeited was only 
$410,000. The forfeiture order did not violate the Exces- 
sive Fines Clause of the Eighth Amendment. 


V 


Defendant raises several other arguments in his pro 
se briefs, mostly related to the issues determined above. 
These arguments are not sufficiently meritorious to war- 
rant their being specifically addressed. 


The order of forfeiture is AFFIRMED. The govern- 
ment’s motion to dismiss is DENIED. Defendant’s motion 
for limited remand is DENIED as moot.
